```text
1	\section{Experimental Setup}
2	\label{sec:methodology}
3	
4	\subsection{Base Architecture}
5	\label{sec:base_arch}
6	
7	All experiments build on DreamerV3 \citep{hafner2023dreamerv3}, which learns a recurrent state-space model (RSSM) with discrete latent states ($32$ categories $\times$ $32$ classes), uses symlog-transformed predictions, and trains an actor-critic entirely within imagined trajectories. The standard configuration imagines $H{=}15$ steps at a fixed temporal resolution of $\dt{=}1$, meaning each imagination step corresponds to one environment transition.
8	
9	\subsection{Variable-Rate Imagination Mechanism}
10	\label{sec:variable_rate}
11	
12	We introduce three orthogonal architectural modifications that enable variable-rate imagination in DreamerV3. Each can be independently toggled to isolate its contribution.
13	
14	\paragraph{Temporal embedding.}
15	A learned embedding $e(\dt)$ is concatenated to the RSSM input at each imagination step to condition state predictions on the temporal scale. We test three modes:
16	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
17	\item \textbf{Trained}: embedding parameters are updated via backpropagation through the world model loss, so the RSSM can co-adapt its dynamics and reward predictions to the temporal scale.
18	\item \textbf{Frozen}: a fixed random projection of identical dimensionality (\texttt{requires\_grad=False}). The RSSM receives a temporal signal but cannot co-adapt to it.
19	\item \textbf{Removed}: embedding dimension set to zero; no temporal conditioning signal is provided.
20	\end{itemize}
21	We further distinguish \textbf{trained-and-exposed} (subsample ON: the RSSM trains on $\dt{>}1$ transitions) from \textbf{trained-but-unexposed} (subsample OFF: $\dt{>}1$ used only during imagination). This four-way classification (trained/exposed, trained/unexposed, frozen, removed) is the key experimental structure of our factorial design.
22	
23	\paragraph{Temporal subsampling.}
24	When enabled, the replay buffer augments training data with variable-$\dt$ transitions by subsampling consecutive frames. Concretely, with a maximum subsampling factor of~2, the RSSM trains on both $\dt{=}1$ and $\dt{=}2$ transitions drawn from the same episodes. When disabled, only standard $\dt{=}1$ transitions are used. This factor controls whether the world model has seen multi-scale dynamics during training.
25	
26	\paragraph{Imagination horizon and step size.}
27	The imagination horizon $H$ sets the number of rollout steps. We test $H{=}15$ (default) and $H{=}8$. The reduced horizon controls for compounding error: $H{=}8$ steps at $\dt{=}2$ covers $16$ physical time steps, comparable to the $15$ physical steps of the default configuration. The parameter \texttt{gate\_fixed\_dt} forces all imagination steps to use a specific $\dt$ value ($1$, $2$, or $4$); this separates the effect of $\dt$-conditioning from any learned gating policy.
28	
29	\subsection{Factorial Design}
30	\label{sec:factorial}
31	
32	We adopt a factorial ablation across four factors: $\dt$-embedding mode (trained / frozen / none), temporal subsampling (on / off), imagination horizon ($H{=}15$ / $H{=}8$), and imagination step size ($\dt{=}1$, $2$, $4$). The design (12 conditions, Table~\ref{tab:factorial_design} in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}) isolates trained $\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure as orthogonal factors. Key configurations use 4 seeds; following \citet{agarwal2021precipice}, we report per-seed trajectories.
33	
34	\subsection{Diagnostic Metrics}
35	\label{sec:metrics}
36	
37	\paragraph{Normalized value-to-eval ratio ($\nve$).}
38	We define $\nve_t = (v/e)_{\text{config},t} / (v/e)_{\text{baseline},t}$, where $v/e$ is the critic's mean imagined value divided by mean evaluation return, and the baseline is the highest-performing seed (exp\_001, seed~1, 505K steps). We restrict to a \textbf{35K+ window} to avoid early-training inflation. The $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold achieves 27/30 accuracy (AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, mean AUROC${=}0.981$, Appendix~Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}).
39	% Note: AUROC computed on original n=29 validation set; exp_059 (s5) correctly classified under same threshold
40	\subsection{Selective Gradient Stopping}
41	\label{sec:sgs_method}
42	
43	To identify which gradient pathway through $e_{\Delta t}$ drives CIVO, we introduce \textit{selective gradient stopping} (SGS): $e_{\Delta t}$ is detached via $\operatorname{sg}(\cdot)$ before selected prediction heads during the backward pass, preventing those loss gradients from updating $e_{\Delta t}$ while preserving its forward-pass contribution. We test three variants: reward-only (exp\_016e), full-pathway (exp\_018, all non-KL gradients blocked), and obs-decoder-only (exp\_025/026). Gradient decomposition using $\nabla_\theta \mathcal{L}_i$ quantifies each pathway's contribution (Appendix~\ref{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}).
44	
45	\subsection{Forward-Pass Interventions}
46	\label{sec:forward_pass_method}
47	
48	We test two forward-pass interventions as representation- and output-level probes: \textbf{TCN} adds Gaussian noise $\mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)$ to $e_{\Delta t}$ during imagination ($\sigma \in \{0.5, 0.2\}$, three environments); \textbf{RAVE} clips imagined values exceeding an EMA anchor ($c \in \{2.0, 1.5\}$).
49	
50	\subsection{Dual-Critic Mechanism Ablation}
51	\label{sec:dual_critic_method}
52	
53	To test whether standard overestimation countermeasures address CIVO, we conduct a $2{\times}2$ factorial: \{single, dual\} $\times$ \{narrow (${\sim}1.2$M), wide (${\sim}2.4$M)\} critic parameters. The dual-critic variant follows TD3 \citep{fujimoto2018td3}: two independent value heads trained on shared RSSM features, with the actor optimizing $\min(v_1, v_2)$. The wide variant doubles hidden dimensions to control for capacity independently of the min operation.
54	
55	\subsection{Environment and Training Details}
56	\label{sec:env_details}
57	
58	All experiments use Walker Walk and Cheetah Run from the DeepMind Control Suite (DMC; \citealt{tassa2018dmcontrol}) with proprioceptive observations, chosen for their contrasting dynamics (6-joint biped vs.\ 2-joint runner). Walker Walk serves as the primary environment with the full factorial design; Cheetah Run serves as a severity-dependent generalization probe: CIVO manifests at $8$--$70\times$ NVE on Cheetah versus $2$--$5\times$ on Walker, testing whether interventions transfer under amplified severity rather than replicating the full factorial. The baseline runs for 500K steps (peak return 970.4); treatments run for 105K steps, sufficient to observe collapse onset. All hyperparameters follow the DreamerV3 defaults \citep{hafner2023dreamerv3} without task-specific tuning ($d{=}32$ temporal embedding; full hyperparameters in Appendix~\ref{sec:hyperparameters}). Training uses a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (${\sim}80$ GPU-hours total). The codebase extends the official DreamerV3 implementation\footnote{\url{https://github.com/danijar/dreamerv3}, accessed March 2026.} with the variable-rate modules described in \S\ref{sec:variable_rate}.
59	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01X6bKAazd5XUD28Y2Abkx7b`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We identify trained $\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor of CIVO in DreamerV3 (27/30, AUROC${=}0.953$; LOEO cross-validated). No tested intervention reliably eliminates CIVO; in pure imagination, dual critics converge ($\rho > 0.999$), rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective. The feedback architecture---MPC re-planning versus imagination-based bootstrapping---is the hypothesized structural factor. Empirical validation on MPC-based architectures (e.g., TD-MPC2) remains future work.
5	
6	\paragraph{Limitations.}
7	Our analysis covers proprioceptive locomotion (DMC) and simulated manipulation (ManiSkill) but does not test pixel-based observations, Atari benchmarks, or real-robot deployments where sensor noise and actuation latency introduce additional confounds.
8	All experiments use DreamerV3's RSSM; whether CIVO manifests in other imagination-based architectures (e.g., IRIS \citep{micheli2023iris} or latent-space planners beyond TD-MPC2) remains open.
9	We characterize CIVO under fixed-$\dt$ subsampling; learned temporal gates warrant separate study.
10	Statistical power with 4 seeds per configuration is limited \citep{colas2018seeds}; the NVE threshold (LOEO AUROC${=}0.981$) may require recalibration for different reward scales.
11	Code: upon acceptance.
12	\label{sec:limitations}
13	
```

</details>

117	\section{NVE Threshold Sensitivity Analysis}
118	\label{sec:appendix_sensitivity}
119	
120	We report the full sensitivity analysis for the NVE diagnostic framework introduced in Section~\ref{sec:results}. The main text reports Task~B2 (any CIVO detection, $\nve \geq 1.5\times$). Here we additionally present Task~B1, which restricts the positive class to \emph{sustained} CIVO, specifically experiments exhibiting persistent, accelerating value overestimation that progresses to performance collapse. Transient CIVO cases (e.g., exp~009b, which self-corrects after a brief excursion above the threshold) are classified as negative in B1.
121	
122	\subsection{Per-Experiment NVE Values}
123	
124	Table~\ref{tab:nve_raw} lists the NVE values at 35K+ training steps for all eleven factorial experiments (including four 008e\_v3 seeds and exp\_059). Experiments are sorted by descending NVE. Experiment 008e\_v3\_s2 exhibits elevated NVE ($3.78\times$) due to Imagination-Reality Divergence (IRD), a mechanistically distinct phenomenon from CIVO, and is classified as negative in both tasks. Experiment 008e\_v3\_s3 exhibits CIVO with NVE $4.02\times$ despite $\Delta t{=}1$ imagination.

149	
150	\subsection{Multi-Threshold Classification Performance}
151	
152	Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1} reports Task~B1 classification metrics across six NVE thresholds. Task~B1 achieves $\text{AUROC} = 1.000$ because the minimum positive NVE ($4.02\times$, exp~008e\_v3\_s3) exceeds the maximum negative NVE ($3.78\times$, exp~008e\_v3\_s2), yielding perfect rank separation. The optimal threshold lies in the interval $(3.78, 4.02]$; we report $\geq 3.9\times$ as a representative value. Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2} provides the corresponding analysis for Task~B2 ($\text{AUROC} = 0.958$, imperfect separation due to 008e\_v3\_s2).

219	
220	\subsection{Threshold Recommendation}
221	
222	For the main diagnostic (Task~B2), we recommend $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ as the operating threshold for three reasons:
223	
224	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
225	\item \textbf{Maximum recall.} The $1.5\times$ threshold achieves 100\% recall, detecting all five CIVO cases including the transient case (009b, $\nve = 1.62\times$). Raising the threshold to $1.7\times$ loses 009b, dropping recall to 80.0\%.
226	\item \textbf{Best F1.} At $1.5\times$, F1 = 90.9\%, the highest among all tested thresholds for B2. The single false positive (008e\_v3\_s2) is mechanistically identifiable as IRD rather than CIVO.
227	\item \textbf{Sensitivity--specificity tradeoff.} Lower thresholds ($1.0\times$) add false positives without gaining true positives. Higher thresholds ($\geq 1.7\times$) sacrifice recall without eliminating the 008e\_v3\_s2 false positive (which requires $\tau > 3.78$).
228	\end{enumerate}
229	
230	For Task~B1 (sustained CIVO only), perfect classification is achievable at $\nve \geq 3.9\times$, but with only $n_{\text{pos}} = 3$ the statistical power remains limited (accuracy 95\% CI: [71.5\%, 100\%]).
231	
232	\subsection{Expanded ROC Analysis}
233	\label{sec:roc_analysis}
234	
235	The original $n{=}16$ analysis (Tables~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1}--\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}) predates the SGS, RAVE, and TCN experiments. We extend the NVE diagnostic evaluation to $n{=}30$ configurations spanning all tested interventions. Figure~\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).
236	
237	\begin{figure}[h]
238	\centering
239	\includegraphics[width=0.7\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_roc_curve.pdf}
240	\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}30$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample, post-hoc). The preset $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ operating point (star) achieves TPR${=}1.00$, FPR${=}0.18$. Three false positives arise from non-CIVO value overestimation: RAVE anchor pollution, obs-decoder SGS side effects, and IRD eval-driven collapse (008e\_v3 seed~2). Threshold calibrated and validated in-sample, post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}
241	% Note: AUROC computed on original n=29 validation set; exp_059 (s5) correctly classified under same threshold
242	\label{fig:nve_roc}
243	\end{figure}
244	
245	We label a configuration as CIVO-positive if and only if (i)~it uses a trained $\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure, and (ii)~NVE exceeds $1.5\times$ with co-occurring entropy collapse. Configurations exhibiting high NVE through other mechanisms (gradient truncation side effects, value anchor pollution) are labeled non-CIVO.
246	
247	The three false positives (FPR${=}0.18$) are mechanistically distinct from CIVO: (1)~RAVE anchor pollution (exp\_028, the EMA anchor becomes inflated, producing overestimation through a different pathway); (2)~obs-decoder SGS-induced value distortion (exp\_025 seed~2, blocking observation decoder gradients damages the RSSM latent space); (3)~IRD eval-driven collapse (exp\_008e\_v3 seed~2, actor entropy \emph{increases} with evaluation collapse, mechanistically distinct from CIVO's entropy \emph{decrease}). All three produce NVE $> 1.5\times$ through non-CIVO mechanisms, indicating that the NVE diagnostic detects value overestimation broadly rather than CIVO specifically---a feature rather than a limitation for practitioners monitoring deployment health. The closest sub-threshold case (008f seed~4, NVE${=}1.41\times$) lacks $\dt$-conditioning entirely ($d{=}0$); its elevated NVE reflects transient value noise that self-corrects without the positive feedback loop characteristic of CIVO. The closest above-threshold positive (008d seed~3, NVE${=}1.53\times$) exhibits the full CIVO causal chain: trained $\dt$-embedding, entropy decrease, and evaluation collapse to 14\% of baseline.
248	
249	\subsection{NVE Normalization Robustness}
250	\label{sec:nve_robustness}
251	
252	To assess sensitivity to baseline seed choice, we extend the single-seed baseline (seed~1, eval 915.7 at 105K) to three additional seeds trained to 505K steps (Table~\ref{tab:baseline_seeds}).
253	
254	\begin{table}[h]
255	\centering
256	\caption{Walker Walk baseline seeds at 105K and 505K steps.}
257	\label{tab:baseline_seeds}
258	\footnotesize
259	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
260	\toprule
261	\textbf{Seed} & \textbf{Eval@105K} & \textbf{Eval@505K} & \textbf{ve@65K} & \textbf{ve@105K} \\
262	\midrule
263	s1 & 915.7 & 970.4 & 0.563 & 0.656 \\
264	s2 & 443.5 & 945.7 & 0.898 & 0.742 \\
265	s3 & 869.0 & 947.7 & --- & 0.664 \\
266	s4 & 922.8 & 938.9 & --- & 0.624 \\
267	\bottomrule
268	\end{tabular}
269	\end{table}
270	
271	Seed~2 exhibits slow convergence (eval 443.5 at 105K, recovering to 945.7 by 512K), confirming non-pathological slow convergence rather than seed failure. Its elevated $v/e$ ratio at 65K (0.898 vs.\ 0.563 for seed~1) reflects the low evaluation denominator rather than pathological value overestimation. Using seed~2 as the sole normalization reference systematically deflates NVE values, causing two marginal CIVO-positive configurations (008d seed~3, peak NVE $1.53\times$; 009b seed~2, peak NVE $1.62\times$) to fall below the $1.5\times$ threshold. Core CIVO cases (peak NVE $> 3\times$) remain correctly classified under all baseline seeds.
272	
273	Single-seed normalization (s1) yields AUROC $= 0.933$ and accuracy 7/8 (87.5\%) on the Walker subset. Four-seed mean normalization yields AUROC $= 0.867$ and accuracy 6/8 (75.0\%): the boundary case exp\_009b flips from $\nve = 1.62\times$ (s1) to $1.23\times$ (4-seed mean), because its peak occurs at 45K when the s1 $v/e$ ratio (0.331) is well below the 4-seed mean (0.436). All high-NVE configurations remain correctly classified (006: $4.84\times \to 4.52\times$; 008d: $4.12\times \to 3.54\times$). The $1.5\times$ threshold thus correctly identifies all strong CIVO cases regardless of baseline choice; sensitivity is confined to configurations within 10\% of the threshold.
274	
275	\paragraph{Normalization conventions.} Walker NVE is normalized against the single-seed baseline (seed~1, exp\_001, 505K steps). Cheetah NVE is normalized against a 5-seed mean baseline (seeds~0--4, each run to ${\sim}$210K steps). The differing seed counts reflect available baseline data; both provide sufficient coverage of the respective experimental windows (Walker: 210K analysis window; Cheetah: 105K analysis window).
276	
277	\paragraph{Limitations.} With $n = 11$ experiments, Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals remain wide (e.g., 90.9\% accuracy has 95\% CI [58.7\%, 99.8\%]). These results should be interpreted as a diagnostic proof-of-concept rather than a validated clinical threshold. Replication across additional environments and seeds is needed to establish tighter bounds. Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full} reports the original $n{=}16$ evaluation set spanning both environments. The expanded analysis ($n{=}30$, including SGS variants, RAVE, and TCN configurations) yields AUROC${=}0.953$ (Figure~\ref{fig:nve_roc}, in-sample, post-hoc). The original $n{=}16$ core analysis yields AUROC${=}0.969$; the expanded $n{=}30$ set includes SGS, RAVE, TCN, and dual-critic configurations. Cross-architecture validation is deferred to future work.
278	
279	\subsection{Leave-One-Environment-Out Cross-Validation}
280	\label{sec:nve_loeo}
281	
282	To assess whether $\theta{=}1.5\times$ generalizes beyond the in-sample evaluation, we perform leave-one-environment-out (LOEO) cross-validation across Walker ($n{=}14$), Cheetah ($n{=}5$), and PickCube ($n{=}2$). For each fold, we hold out all experiments from one environment, select the optimal accuracy-maximizing threshold range on the remaining training set, and evaluate the fixed $\theta{=}1.5\times$ on the held-out environment.
283	
284	\begin{table}[h]
285	\centering

287	For each fold, the threshold is validated on an environment whose data was \emph{not} used
288	to select $\theta$. The optimal threshold range on each training set is reported;
289	$\theta=1.5$ falls within the optimal range in all three folds.}

The single cross-validation error is exp\_008e\_v3 seed~2 (Walker), an IRD collapse mechanistically distinct from CIVO (\S\ref{sec:roc_analysis}). Mean held-out accuracy is 0.976 and mean AUROC is 0.981, confirming that $\theta{=}1.5\times$ generalizes across environments without retuning.

314	\paragraph{Embedding gradient analysis.}
315	Table~\ref{tab:three_way_emb} isolates embedding \emph{trainability} as the necessary condition for CIVO by comparing four gradient conditions under otherwise identical configurations ($H{=}8$, subsample ON, $\dt{=}2$ imagination).
316

332	\end{tabular}
333	\vspace{1pt}
334	{\raggedright\footnotesize $^*$Full-SGS seed~1 (NVE $1.66\times$); seed~2 collapsed. $^\dagger$Self-corrected at ${\sim}175$K (eval 44$\to$538, 59\% baseline; 224K run). $^\ddagger$Exp\_047 (NVE $1.71\times$) and Exp\_057 (NVE $1.88\times$): both transient, self-correcting within 20K steps.\par}
335	\end{table}
336	
337	The reward-detach condition (exp\_044, \texttt{reward\_dt\_emb\_detach=True}) uses a backward hook to block reward-head gradients from reaching $e_{\dt}$, complementing the framework-level Reward SGS (exp\_016e). Both yield the same directional result---CIVO onset not prevented---ruling out implementation artifacts. Exp\_044 exhibits delayed CIVO onset (${\sim}95$K vs.\ typical $25$--$35$K), consistent with reduced but non-zero gradient pressure on $e_{\dt}$. Over a full 224K run, exp\_044 self-corrects: eval recovers from 44 (95K) to 538 (215K, 59\% baseline), but this recovery is incomplete compared to spontaneous recovery without gradient intervention (exp\_045: 868, 95\% baseline). The lower asymptote suggests that selectively disrupting gradient flow may create optimization difficulties beyond CIVO itself.
338	
339	Table~\ref{tab:factorial_design} summarizes the experimental configurations.
340	
341	\begin{table}[ht]
342	\centering
343	\caption{Factorial design. Each row specifies one experimental configuration. \textbf{Emb}: $\dt$-embedding mode. \textbf{Sub}: temporal subsampling. \textbf{H}: imagination horizon. \textbf{Imag $\dt$}: temporal resolution used during imagination.}
344	\label{tab:factorial_design}
345	\small
346	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}
347	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}
348	\toprule
349	\textbf{ID} & \textbf{Purpose} & \textbf{Emb} & \textbf{Sub} & \textbf{H} & \textbf{Imag $\dt$} \\
350	\midrule
351	001 & Reference baseline & --- & --- & 15 & 1 \\
352	006 & Fixed $\dt{=}2$ & trn & on & 15 & 2 \\
353	007 & Fixed $\dt{=}4$ & trn & on & 15 & 4 \\
354	\midrule
355	008a & Unexposed emb ctrl & exp$^a$ & off & 15 & 2 \\
356	008c & Horizon control & exp$^a$ & off & 8 & 2 \\
357	008d & Collapse condition & trn & on & 8 & 2 \\
358	008e\_v3 & $\dt{=}1$ imag control & trn & on & 15 & 1 \\
359	008f & Causal ablation & --- & on & 8 & 2 \\
360	\midrule
361	015b & Frozen-emb ctrl & frz$^b$ & on & 8 & 2 \\
362	016e & Stop-grad (reward) & trn$^\dagger$ & on & 8 & 2 \\
363	018 & Full-SGS & trn$^\ddagger$ & on & 8 & 2 \\

371	exp$^a$ = trained ($d{=}32$) but never exposed to $\dt{>}1$ data (subsample OFF);
372	frz$^b$ = frozen random projection ($d{=}32$, \texttt{requires\_grad=False});

376	
377	\subsection{F3: Unexposed Temporal Embedding Suppresses Value Drift}
378	\label{sec:f3}
379	
380	\begin{figure}[t]
381	\centering
382	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_factorial_grid.pdf}
383	\caption{Factorial isolation of CIVO trigger. Only the combination of trained $e_{\dt}$ and multi-rate data (upper-left cell) produces pathological NVE. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}
384	\label{fig:factorial_grid}
385	\end{figure}
386	
387	The empirical finding that trained embedding \emph{plus} multi-rate exposure jointly predict CIVO rests on two complementary controls that each remove one factor while holding the other constant.
388	
389	\paragraph{Evidence A: exposure is necessary (exp\_008a).}
390	Exp\_008a uses a trained embedding ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) that is never exposed to $\dt{>}1$ transitions during world-model training (subsample OFF). Despite sharing the same embedding architecture as exp\_008d, its NVE remains in the $0.83$--$0.93\times$ range across 3 seeds (105K steps). Performance follows two V-shaped recovery cycles, reaching 777.7 at 105K (85\% of baseline). The critical factor is not the embedding's capacity to represent temporal information, but its adaptation to multi-rate data that enables precise but inaccurate $\dt{>}1$ state predictions within the RSSM.
391	
392	This contrasts with exp\_006 (trained $e_{\dt}$, subsample ON, $H{=}15$), where NVE accelerates from $1.61\times$ at 45K to $2.31\times$ at 55K before catastrophic collapse to 132.9 at 65K. Both experiments use identical trained embeddings ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) and $H{=}15$, differing only in whether the world model sees $\dt{>}1$ transitions during training. This confirms that multi-rate data exposure, not embedding architecture, is the operative variable.
393	
394	\paragraph{Evidence B: trainability is necessary (exp\_015b).}
395	Exp\_015b uses a frozen random-projection embedding ($d{=}32$, \texttt{requires\_grad=False}, subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the same multi-rate data regime as exp\_008d, but with a non-trainable temporal signal. Despite full exposure to $\dt{>}1$ transitions, the NVE peaks at $1.45\times$ at 55K but does not sustain escalation: it retreats to $0.65\times$ by 65K and settles at $0.42\times$ by 105K (trajectory: $0.81 \rightarrow 1.26 \rightarrow 1.45 \rightarrow 0.65 \rightarrow 1.05 \rightarrow 0.47 \rightarrow 0.39 \rightarrow 0.42$). The transient peak approaches but does not exceed the $1.5\times$ CIVO threshold, and critically, the ratio self-corrects rather than exhibiting the monotonic escalation seen in trained-embedding runs. Final evaluation reaches only 204.2 at 105K, low but through a qualitatively different failure mode than CIVO. Actor loss approaches zero (gradient starvation; see \S\ref{sec:pathways}), the frozen embedding provides no useful gradient signal for temporal adaptation.
396	
397	The contrast with exp\_008d is stark: under identical data conditions (subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the trained embedding drives NVE to $4.12\times$ with sustained monotonic escalation ($1.78 \rightarrow 1.71 \rightarrow 2.09 \rightarrow 4.12$) and precedes full collapse (eval 82.8), while the frozen embedding peaks transiently at $1.45\times$ before self-correcting to $0.42\times$, a qualitative difference between sustained and transient overestimation. This demonstrates that a $\dt$-embedding must be able to \emph{co-adapt} with RSSM dynamics to produce the systematic reward-prediction bias that drives CIVO; a static random projection, regardless of its exposure to multi-rate data, cannot create this bias.
398	
399	Together, these two controls establish that in all tested configurations, CIVO occurs only with the \emph{joint} presence of (i)~a trainable embedding that can co-adapt with RSSM dynamics, and (ii)~multi-rate training data that drives this co-adaptation toward biased temporal predictions. Neither factor alone is sufficient.
```

Includes Walker Walk (15 configurations) and Cheetah Run (2 configurations).}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nThreshold & TP & FP & FN & TN & Accuracy [95\\% CI] & Precision & Recall & F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\geq 1.0\\times$ & 9 & 2 & 0 & 6 & 88.2\\% \\; [63.6, 98.5] & 81.8\\% & 100\\% & 90.0\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.2\\times$ & 9 & 1 & 0 & 7 & 94.1\\% \\; [71.3, 99.9] & 90.0\\% & 100\\% & 94.7\\% \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n$\\geq 1.5\\times$ & 9 & 1 & 0 & 7 & 94.1\\% \\; [71.3, 99.9] & 90.0\\% & 100\\% & 94.7\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.7\\times$ & 7 & 1 & 2 & 7 & 82.4\\% \\; [56.6, 96.2] & 87.5\\% & 77.8\\% & 82.4\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 2.0\\times$ & 7 & 1 & 2 & 7 & 82.4\\% \\; [56.6, 96.2] & 87.5\\% & 77.8\\% & 82.4\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 3.9\\times$ & 7 & 0 & 2 & 8 & 88.2\\% \\; [63.6, 98.5] & 100\\% & 77.8\\% & 87.5\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Threshold Recommendation}\n\nFor the main diagnostic (Task~B2), we recommend $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ as the operating threshold for three reasons:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Maximum recall.} The $1.5\\times$ threshold achieves 100\\% recall, detecting all five CIVO cases including the transient case (009b, $\\nve = 1.62\\times$). Raising the threshold to $1.7\\times$ loses 009b, dropping recall to 80.0\\%.\n\\item \\textbf{Best F1.} At $1.5\\times$, F1 = 90.9\\%, the highest among all tested thresholds for B2. The single false positive (008e\\_v3\\_s2) is mechanistically identifiable as IRD rather than CIVO.\n\\item \\textbf{Sensitivity--specificity tradeoff.} Lower thresholds ($1.0\\times$) add false positives without gaining true positives. Higher thresholds ($\\geq 1.7\\times$) sacrifice recall without eliminating the 008e\\_v3\\_s2 false positive (which requires $\\tau > 3.78$).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nFor Task~B1 (sustained CIVO only), perfect classification is achievable at $\\nve \\geq 3.9\\times$, but with only $n_{\\text{pos}} = 3$ the statistical power remains limited (accuracy 95\\% CI: [71.5\\%, 100\\%]).\n\n\\subsection{Expanded ROC Analysis}\n\\label{sec:roc_analysis}\n\nThe original $n{=}16$ analysis (Tables~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1}--\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}) predates the SGS, RAVE, and TCN experiments. We extend the NVE diagnostic evaluation to $n{=}30$ configurations spanning all tested interventions. Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.7\\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_roc_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}30$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample, post-hoc). The preset $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ operating point (star) achieves TPR${=}1.00$, FPR${=}0.18$. Three false positives arise from non-CIVO value overestimation: RAVE anchor pollution, obs-decoder SGS side effects, and IRD eval-driven collapse (008e\\_v3 seed~2). Threshold calibrated and validated in-sample, post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}\n% Note: AUROC computed on original n=29 validation set; exp_059 (s5) correctly classified under same threshold\n\\label{fig:nve_roc}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe label a configuration as CIVO-positive if and only if (i)~it uses a trained $\\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure, and (ii)~NVE exceeds $1.5\\times$ with co-occurring entropy collapse. Configurations exhibiting high NVE through other mechanisms (gradient truncation side effects, value anchor pollution) are labeled non-CIVO.\n\nThe three false positives (FPR${=}0.18$) are mechanistically distinct from CIVO: (1)~RAVE anchor pollution (exp\\_028, the EMA anchor becomes inflated, producing overestimation through a different pathway); (2)~obs-decoder SGS-induced value distortion (exp\\_025 seed~2, blocking observation decoder gradients damages the RSSM latent space); (3)~IRD eval-driven collapse (exp\\_008e\\_v3 seed~2, actor entropy \\emph{increases} with evaluation collapse, mechanistically distinct from CIVO's entropy \\emph{decrease}). All three produce NVE $> 1.5\\times$ through non-CIVO mechanisms, indicating that the NVE diagnostic detects value overestimation broadly rather than CIVO specifically---a feature rather than a limitation for practitioners monitoring deployment health. The closest sub-threshold case (008f seed~4, NVE${=}1.41\\times$) lacks $\\dt$-conditioning entirely ($d{=}0$); its elevated NVE reflects transient value noise that self-corrects without the positive feedback loop characteristic of CIVO. The closest above-threshold positive (008d seed~3, NVE${=}1.53\\times$) exhibits the full CIVO causal chain: trained $\\dt$-embedding, entropy decrease, and evaluation collapse to 14\\% of baseline.\n\n\\subsection{NVE Normalization Robustness}\n\\label{sec:nve_robustness}\n\nTo assess sensitivity to baseline seed choice, we extend the single-seed baseline (seed~1, eval 915.7 at 105K) to three additional seeds trained to 505K steps (Table~\\ref{tab:baseline_seeds}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Walker Walk baseline seeds at 105K and 505K steps.}\n\\label{tab:baseline_seeds}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{Eval@105K} & \\textbf{Eval@505K} & \\textbf{ve@65K} & \\textbf{ve@105K} \\\\\n\\midrule\ns1 & 915.7 & 970.4 & 0.563 & 0.656 \\\\\ns2 & 443.5 & 945.7 & 0.898 & 0.742 \\\\\ns3 & 869.0 & 947.7 & --- & 0.664 \\\\\ns4 & 922.8 & 938.9 & --- & 0.624 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nSeed~2 exhibits slow convergence (eval 443.5 at 105K, recovering to 945.7 by 512K), confirming non-pathological slow convergence rather than seed failure. Its elevated $v/e$ ratio at 65K (0.898 vs.\\ 0.563 for seed~1) reflects the low evaluation denominator rather than pathological value overestimation. Using seed~2 as the sole normalization reference systematically deflates NVE values, causing two marginal CIVO-positive configurations (008d seed~3, peak NVE $1.53\\times$; 009b seed~2, peak NVE $1.62\\times$) to fall below the $1.5\\times$ threshold. Core CIVO cases (peak NVE $> 3\\times$) remain correctly classified under all baseline seeds.\n\nSingle-seed normalization (s1) yields AUROC $= 0.933$ and accuracy 7/8 (87.5\\%) on the Walker subset. Four-seed mean normalization yields AUROC $= 0.867$ and accuracy 6/8 (75.0\\%): the boundary case exp\\_009b flips from $\\nve = 1.62\\times$ (s1) to $1.23\\times$ (4-seed mean), because its peak occurs at 45K when the s1 $v/e$ ratio (0.331) is well below the 4-seed mean (0.436). All high-NVE configurations remain correctly classified (006: $4.84\\times \\to 4.52\\times$; 008d: $4.12\\times \\to 3.54\\times$). The $1.5\\times$ threshold thus correctly identifies all strong CIVO cases regardless of baseline choice; sensitivity is confined to configurations within 10\\% of the threshold.\n\n\\paragraph{Normalization conventions.} Walker NVE is normalized against the single-seed baseline (seed~1, exp\\_001, 505K steps). Cheetah NVE is normalized against a 5-seed mean baseline (seeds~0--4, each run to ${\\sim}$210K steps). The differing seed counts reflect available baseline data; both provide sufficient coverage of the respective experimental windows (Walker: 210K analysis window; Cheetah: 105K analysis window).\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} With $n = 11$ experiments, Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals remain wide (e.g., 90.9\\% accuracy has 95\\% CI [58.7\\%, 99.8\\%]). These results should be interpreted as a diagnostic proof-of-concept rather than a validated clinical threshold. The expanded analysis ($n{=}30$, including SGS variants, RAVE, and TCN configurations) yields AUROC${=}0.953$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc}, in-sample, post-hoc). The original $n{=}16$ core analysis yields AUROC${=}0.969$; the expanded $n{=}30$ set includes SGS, RAVE, TCN, and dual-critic configurations. Cross-architecture validation is deferred to future work.\n\n\\subsection{Leave-One-Environment-Out Cross-Validation}\n\\label{sec:nve_loeo}\n\nTo assess whether $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ generalizes beyond the in-sample evaluation, we perform leave-one-environment-out (LOEO) cross-validation across Walker ($n{=}14$), Cheetah ($n{=}5$), and PickCube ($n{=}2$). For each fold, we hold out all experiments from one environment, select the optimal accuracy-maximizing threshold range on the remaining training set, and evaluate the fixed $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ on the held-out environment.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Leave-one-environment-out cross-validation of NVE threshold $\\theta=1.5\\times$.\nFor each fold, the threshold is validated on an environment whose data was \\emph{not} used\nto select $\\theta$. 446	
447	\section{Full Analysis \& Discussion}
448	\label{sec:appendix_analysis}
449	
450	This appendix provides the complete mechanistic analysis summarized in Section~\ref{sec:discussion} of the main text.
451	
452	\subsection{The CIVO Mechanism}
453	\label{sec:civo_mechanism}
454	
455	\begin{figure}[t]
456	\centering
457	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig4_mechanism_diagram.pdf}
458	\caption{CIVO mechanism diagram. The trained $\dt$-embedding enables asymmetric reward prediction fidelity, initiating a feedback loop: biased RSSM rewards $\to$ optimistic critic values $\to$ actor exploitation $\to$ reinforced state-distribution skew.}
459	\label{fig:civo_mechanism}
460	\end{figure}
461	
462	\begin{table}[h]
463	\centering
464	\caption{RSSM fidelity at $\dt{=}1$ vs.\ $\dt{=}2$. Trained embedding (008d) produces $2.78\times$ reward gap; removing it (008f) eliminates the gap but degrades absolute prediction.}
465	\label{tab:rssm_fidelity}
466	\footnotesize
467	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
468	\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}
469	\toprule
470	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{obs\_MSE}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{reward\_MAE}} \\
471	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-7}
472	\textbf{Config} & $\dt{=}1$ & $\dt{=}2$ & Gap & $\dt{=}1$ & $\dt{=}2$ & Gap \\
473	\midrule
474	Baseline & 13.49 & 27.50 & 2.04$\times$ & 0.0102 & 0.0159 & 1.56$\times$ \\
475	008d (trained) & 41.31 & 53.67 & 1.30$\times$ & 0.0568 & 0.1579 & 2.78$\times$ \\
476	008f (no emb) & 8.01 & 22.04 & 2.75$\times$ & 0.7469 & 0.7526 & 1.01$\times$ \\
477	\bottomrule
478	\end{tabular}
479	\end{table}
480	
481	The RSSM fidelity analysis (Table~\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) reveals the mechanistic core of CIVO (Figure~\ref{fig:civo_mechanism}).  The trained $\dt$-embedding enables the reward head to learn temporally differentiated predictions, but these predictions are asymmetrically accurate: reward MAE at $\dt{=}1$ is $0.0568$, while at $\dt{=}2$ it rises to $0.1579$, a $2.78\times$ gap.  This gap is not an artifact of prediction difficulty alone; the no-embedding configuration (008f) exhibits a $1.01\times$ gap, and the baseline shows $1.56\times$.  The trained embedding actively amplifies the fidelity discrepancy by allowing the reward head to co-adapt with the temporal signal.
482	
483	This asymmetric fidelity initiates a feedback loop with four stages: (1) the trained $\dt$-embedding causes RSSM predictions at $\dt > 1$ to carry systematically biased reward estimates; (2) the critic, trained entirely on imagined trajectories, internalizes these biased rewards as optimistic value estimates; (3) the actor exploits the overestimated regions of the value landscape, shifting its policy toward states that appear high-value only under the biased model; and (4) this policy shift further skews the distribution of imagined states, reinforcing the bias.  The diagnostic signature is a sustained $\nve$ above $1.5\times$, followed by evaluation collapse.
484	
485	A random-policy control experiment ($N{=}100$ rollouts, 95\% bootstrap CI) decomposes CIVO into two necessary stages.  \textbf{Stage~1 (dynamics bias):} even under a random policy, $\dt{=}2$ imagination produces $1.68\times$ higher mean reward than $\dt{=}1$ in the trained-embedding model (008d): the RSSM prior already generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\dt > 1$, independent of actor behavior.  \textbf{Stage~2 (actor exploitation):} the trained actor learns to exploit these biased regions, amplifying the ratio to $2.90\times$.  In the no-embedding control (008f), neither stage is present: the random-policy ratio is $1.11\times$ and the trained-actor ratio is $0.98\times$.  The decomposition attributes $\approx 36\%$ of the total effect to dynamics bias and $\approx 64\%$ to actor exploitation, which confirms that both stages are necessary for CIVO to manifest.
486	
487	This mechanism is analogous to delusional bias \citep{lu2018nondelusional}, but differs in origin: delusional bias arises from policy-class constraints, while CIVO is model-induced.  The consequence (policy exploitation of overestimated regions) is similar, but the causal pathway is distinct.
488	
489	\paragraph{Signed reward error analysis.}
490	To test whether CIVO arises from one-step reward overprediction, we compute signed error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions (Table~\ref{tab:signed_error}). The trained-embedding model (008d) shows \emph{negative} bias at $\dt{=}2$ (mean signed error $-0.599$, only 8\% of samples overpredicted), ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the mechanism. The baseline is well-calibrated (mean SE $\approx 0$), confirming methodological soundness. Value overestimation therefore occurs during \emph{imagination-level} prior drift: the RSSM prior (unconditioned on observations) generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\dt > 1$ (Stage~1 above, $1.68\times$ ratio under random policy), and the critic accumulates these into overestimated returns. The one-step reward prediction is negatively biased when conditioned on real observations, but this posterior correction is unavailable during imagination. This also distinguishes CIVO from generic compounding error, which would produce \emph{positive} signed error accumulating with horizon; the negative observation-level bias confirms that CIVO is a prior-drift phenomenon, not a reward-prediction artifact.
491

494	\caption{Signed reward error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions. Negative values indicate underprediction. The trained-embedding model underpredicts at $\dt{=}2$, ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the CIVO mechanism.}

496	\footnotesize

512	
513	\subsection{Selective Gradient Stopping and Gradient Decomposition}
514	\label{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}
515	
516	\subsubsection{Gradient Decomposition Results}
517	\label{sec:gradient_decomposition}
518	
519	\begin{figure}[t]
520	\centering
521	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_gradient_decomposition.pdf}
522	\caption{Gradient contribution to $e_{\Delta t}$ by loss component at two training checkpoints. The reward head contributes the largest share (38--49\%) yet reward-only SGS (exp\_016e) fails; the observation decoder channel (16--22\%) carries the critical $\dt$-specific spatial information that drives the RSSM fidelity gap.}
523	\label{fig:gradient_decomp}
524	\end{figure}
525	
526	Exp\_016e collapses in the same qualitative pattern as the unmodified control: peak evaluation 326.7 at 75K steps followed by catastrophic decline to 31.7 at 95K, with NVE reaching $10.42\times$. The SGS intervention neither prevented nor attenuated CIVO.
527	
528	To explain this null result, we performed a gradient decomposition analysis using \texttt{torch.autograd.grad} to measure each loss component's contribution to $\nabla_\theta e_{\Delta t}$, where $\theta$ denotes the temporal embedding parameters. Table~\ref{tab:gradient_decomposition} reports the results.
529	
530	\begin{table}[ht]
531	\centering
532	\caption{Gradient contributions to the temporal embedding $e_{\Delta t}$ by loss component, measured via \texttt{torch.autograd.grad} with training configuration \texttt{gate\_fixed\_dt=2}. Gradient norms are $\ell_2$ norms of $\nabla_\theta \mathcal{L}_i$ for each loss $\mathcal{L}_i$.}
533	\label{tab:gradient_decomposition}
534	\small
535	\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
536	\toprule
537	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{@55K}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{@112K}} \\
538	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5}
539	Loss component & Share & (\%) & Share & (\%) \\
540	\midrule
541	Reward head & --- & 49.0 & --- & 38.1 \\
542	KL dynamics & --- & 34.7 & --- & 40.4 \\
543	Observation decoders & --- & 16.3 & --- & 21.5 \\
544	\bottomrule
545	\end{tabular}
546	\end{table}
547	
548	The gradient decomposition reveals a counterintuitive structure: the reward head provides the largest gradient contribution (49.0\% at 55K, 38.1\% at 112K), yet reward-only SGS (exp\_016e) fails catastrophically (NVE $10.42\times$). The observation decoder channel contributes the smallest share (16.3\% at 55K, 21.5\% at 112K) yet carries the critical $\dt$-specific spatial information.  This dissociation between gradient magnitude and causal importance explains why exp\_016e, which targets the largest pathway, is functionally insufficient for CIVO prevention.
549	
550	The gradient structure is relatively stable across training (reward shifts from 49\% to 38\%, obs from 16\% to 22\%, KL from 35\% to 40\%), unlike what a magnitude-based account would predict. The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects the scalar reward prediction task's sensitivity to temporal embedding parameters, but this gradient does not encode $\Delta t$-specific structural information about spatial dynamics. The observation decoder channel, despite its smaller magnitude, is the only pathway that encodes how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales.
551	
552	\paragraph{Why the smallest channel is causally critical.} The observation decoder is the only loss component whose gradients encode how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states ($14$ orientations, $9$ velocities, $1$ height) across temporal scales.  This $\dt$-specific spatial information shapes the latent state representation in ways that propagate to reward prediction: the RSSM fidelity gap ($2.78\times$ reward MAE ratio, Table~\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) arises because the temporal embedding learns to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\dt{>}1$, not because the reward head directly learns scale-dependent biases.  Full-SGS targets this causal chain by preventing observation-decoder gradients from updating $e_{\Delta t}$, though its 0/2 CIVO elimination rate on Walker (seed~1: NVE reduced from $4.12\times$ to $1.66\times$ but remains CIVO-positive; seed~2: collapsed) indicates that breaking the gradient pathway alone does not fully eliminate the vulnerability.
553	
554	\subsubsection{Implications for the CIVO Mechanism}
555	\label{sec:sgs_implications}
556	
557	The gradient decomposition reveals that gradient magnitude and causal importance are decoupled in CIVO.
558	
559	\paragraph{Gradient importance $\neq$ gradient magnitude.} The reward head contributes the largest gradient share (38--49\%) to $e_{\Delta t}$, yet reward-only SGS fails.  The observation decoder contributes the smallest share (16--22\%), yet its removal (via Full-SGS) is necessary for CIVO mitigation.  This dissociation arises because the reward head's gradient, while large, does not encode $\dt$-specific structural information: it adjusts $e_{\Delta t}$ to minimize a scalar prediction error that is largely $\dt$-invariant.  The observation decoder's gradient, though smaller, encodes how the RSSM should differentially reconstruct 24-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales. This is the channel through which $e_{\Delta t}$ learns scale-dependent latent biases.
560	
561	\paragraph{Reframing the intervention target.} Any gradient-level strategy that targets the reward head alone (reward prediction regularization, reward-conditioned value clipping, or reward-only SGS) targets the largest pathway but not the informationally critical one.  The failure of exp\_016e (NVE $10.42\times$) despite detaching the dominant gradient source confirms this.  Full-pathway SGS (exp\_018) detaches $e_{\Delta t}$ from all non-KL gradients, retaining only the KL dynamics contribution (34--40\%).  However, this intervention eliminates CIVO in neither seed: seed~1 shows partial mitigation (eval 448.3, peak NVE $1.66\times$, still CIVO-positive) while seed~2 collapses (eval 40.8). Removing the observation decoder gradient channel is thus contributory but not sufficient. Figure~\ref{fig:civo_decomposition} decomposes evaluation returns across conditions.
562	
563	\begin{figure}[ht]
564	\centering
565	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_civo_decomposition.pdf}
566	\caption{CIVO decomposition: peak and final (105K step) evaluation returns on Walker Walk across four conditions. \textbf{Baseline} (001): vanilla DreamerV3. \textbf{Trained dt-emb} (008d)\protect\footnotemark: learned $d{=}32$ temporal embedding with multi-rate data. \textbf{Full-SGS} (s1, s2): trained embedding with all non-KL gradients detached from $e_{\dt}$. Trained $\dt$-embedding degrades peak eval by $73\%$ and final eval by $86\%$. Full-SGS seed~1 shows partial mitigation ($-51\%$ final, NVE $1.66\times$, still CIVO-positive) while seed~2 collapses ($-96\%$ final). CIVO elimination rate $= 0/2$.}
567	\label{fig:civo_decomposition}
568	\end{figure}
569	\footnotetext{Experiment 008d terminated at 55K steps; the 105K data point uses continuation run 009e under identical configuration.}
570	
571	Cross-environment validation on Cheetah Run (exp\_021) further clarifies the role of Full-SGS.  The intervention did not prevent the initial CIVO onset: NVE peaked at $9.94\times$ at 45K (comparable to the unmitigated exp\_010b's $8.27\times$).  However, unlike exp\_010b, which collapsed permanently to eval 51.7, exp\_021 self-corrected within ${\sim}$10K steps, with sustained NVE dropping to $0.34\times$ (55K--105K) and final evaluation recovering to 221.5.  This transient self-correcting trajectory is not attributable to the SGS intervention: the initial CIVO onset and peak severity are comparable to the unmitigated case, and the self-correction may reflect Cheetah Run's simpler dynamics rather than SGS efficacy.  Performance remains at 38.7\% of baseline (572.5), likely because Full-SGS over-aggressively blocks actor gradients (actor gradient norms near zero throughout training).  Combined with exp\_018's Walker results (0/2 CIVO elimination), these findings yield a 0/3 elimination rate across environments, indicating that gradient-level interventions alone do not eliminate the CIVO vulnerability. Additional mechanisms (e.g., forward-pass conditioning effects, residual capacity-dependent instabilities) operate beyond the gradient pathway.
572	
573	\paragraph{Connection to RSSM fidelity analysis.} The trained-embedding condition (exp\_008d) shows a $2.78\times$ reward gap between $\Delta t{=}1$ and $\Delta t{=}2$ transitions (Table~\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}).  This gap arises because the observation decoder gradients shape $e_{\Delta t}$ to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\dt{>}1$, and the reward head inherits these biases \textit{through the latent state} rather than learning them directly.  The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects sensitivity to $e_{\Delta t}$'s parameters but not causal responsibility for the fidelity gap.
574

576	\label{sec:entropy_dynamics}

The CIVO-triggering configuration (008d, red) follows a normal entropy reduction trajectory; the divergence from the control (008f) is subtle in entropy space, consistent with CIVO operating through the value channel rather than the entropy channel. 018v2 (Full-SGS, seed~1) shows slightly lower terminal entropy.}
582	\label{fig:entropy_dynamics}
583	\end{figure}
584	
585	\subsubsection{Exp\_016e Reinterpreted as Stochastic Variation}
586	\label{sec:016e_reinterp}
587	
588	The gradient decomposition establishes that exp\_016e targets the largest gradient pathway (reward: 38--49\%) but not the informationally critical one (observation decoders: 16--22\%).  The apparent timing differences between exp\_016e and exp\_008d (later collapse onset, 75K vs.\ 25K, and higher peak evaluation, 326.7 vs.\ 167.2) must be attributed to stochastic variation rather than SGS effects, since the critical observation decoder channel remains intact.  All three trajectories (exp\_008d, exp\_009b, exp\_016e) share the invariant identified by the NVE diagnostic: each crosses the $1.5\times$ pathological threshold at some point during training.

From the $\\dt{=}1$ baseline (915.7 at 105K), fixed $\\dt{=}2$ (exp\\_006) achieves a peak of only 485.3 ($-47\\%$), and fixed $\\dt{=}4$ (exp\\_007) peaks at 176.9 ($-81\\%$). This monotonic relationship is consistent with compounding model error: larger temporal gaps amplify the RSSM's prediction inaccuracy at each imagination step, and these errors accumulate across the rollout horizon.\n\n\\subsection{F5: Imagination Horizon and Subsampling Are Secondary Factors}\n\\label{sec:f5}\n\nNeither horizon length nor temporal subsampling precedes collapse without multi-rate data exposure: both trained-but-unexposed configurations (008a, 008c) maintain $\\nve < 0.93\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:f3}), while the trained-and-exposed counterpart (008d) collapses with $\\nve = 4.12\\times$.  The no-embedding configurations show cross-seed variance (008f: 503.0 vs.\\ 009c: 148.3) but neither exhibits $\\nve > 1.5\\times$; low evaluation alone does not indicate CIVO.\n\nThe $3.2\\times$ performance gap (008a: 777.7 vs.\\ 008c: 240.9) quantifies the horizon effect independently of CIVO, as both conditions maintain healthy NVE ($\\leq$0.93$\\times$).\n\n% --- APPENDIX C: Full Analysis & Discussion ---\n\n\\section{Full Analysis \\& Discussion}\n\\label{sec:appendix_analysis}\n\nThis appendix provides the complete mechanistic analysis summarized in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion} of the main text.\n\n\\subsection{The CIVO Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:civo_mechanism}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig4_mechanism_diagram.pdf}\n\\caption{CIVO mechanism diagram. The trained $\\dt$-embedding enables asymmetric reward prediction fidelity, initiating a feedback loop: biased RSSM rewards $\\to$ optimistic critic values $\\to$ actor exploitation $\\to$ reinforced state-distribution skew.}\n\\label{fig:civo_mechanism}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{RSSM fidelity at $\\dt{=}1$ vs.\\ $\\dt{=}2$. Trained embedding (008d) produces $2.78\\times$ reward gap; removing it (008f) eliminates the gap but degrades absolute prediction.}\n\\label{tab:rssm_fidelity}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{obs\\_MSE}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{reward\\_MAE}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Config} & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline & 13.49 & 27.50 & 2.04$\\times$ & 0.0102 & 0.0159 & 1.56$\\times$ \\\\\n008d (trained) & 41.31 & 53.67 & 1.30$\\times$ & 0.0568 & 0.1579 & 2.78$\\times$ \\\\\n008f (no emb) & 8.01 & 22.04 & 2.75$\\times$ & 0.7469 & 0.7526 & 1.01$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe RSSM fidelity analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) reveals the mechanistic core of CIVO (Figure~\\ref{fig:civo_mechanism}).  The trained $\\dt$-embedding enables the reward head to learn temporally differentiated predictions, but these predictions are asymmetrically accurate: reward MAE at $\\dt{=}1$ is $0.0568$, while at $\\dt{=}2$ it rises to $0.1579$, a $2.78\\times$ gap.  This gap is not an artifact of prediction difficulty alone; the no-embedding configuration (008f) exhibits a $1.01\\times$ gap, and the baseline shows $1.56\\times$.  The trained embedding actively amplifies the fidelity discrepancy by allowing the reward head to co-adapt with the temporal signal.\n\nThis asymmetric fidelity initiates a feedback loop with four stages: (1) the trained $\\dt$-embedding causes RSSM predictions at $\\dt > 1$ to carry systematically biased reward estimates; (2) the critic, trained entirely on imagined trajectories, internalizes these biased rewards as optimistic value estimates; (3) the actor exploits the overestimated regions of the value landscape, shifting its policy toward states that appear high-value only under the biased model; and (4) this policy shift further skews the distribution of imagined states, reinforcing the bias.  The diagnostic signature is a sustained $\\nve$ above $1.5\\times$, followed by evaluation collapse.\n\nA random-policy control experiment ($N{=}100$ rollouts, 95\\% bootstrap CI) decomposes CIVO into two necessary stages.  \\textbf{Stage~1 (dynamics bias):} even under a random policy, $\\dt{=}2$ imagination produces $1.68\\times$ higher mean reward than $\\dt{=}1$ in the trained-embedding model (008d): the RSSM prior already generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\\dt > 1$, independent of actor behavior.  \\textbf{Stage~2 (actor exploitation):} the trained actor learns to exploit these biased regions, amplifying the ratio to $2.90\\times$.  In the no-embedding control (008f), neither stage is present: the random-policy ratio is $1.11\\times$ and the trained-actor ratio is $0.98\\times$.  The decomposition attributes $\\approx 36\\%$ of the total effect to dynamics bias and $\\approx 64\\%$ to actor exploitation, which confirms that both stages are necessary for CIVO to manifest.\n\nThis mechanism is analogous to delusional bias \\citep{lu2018nondelusional}, but differs in origin: delusional bias arises from policy-class constraints, while CIVO is model-induced.  The consequence (policy exploitation of overestimated regions) is similar, but the causal pathway is distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Signed reward error analysis.}\nTo test whether CIVO arises from one-step reward overprediction, we compute signed error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions (Table~\\ref{tab:signed_error}). The trained-embedding model (008d) shows \\emph{negative} bias at $\\dt{=}2$ (mean signed error $-0.599$, only 8\\% of samples overpredicted), ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the mechanism. The baseline is well-calibrated (mean SE $\\approx 0$), confirming methodological soundness. Value overestimation therefore occurs during \\emph{imagination-level} prior drift: the RSSM prior (unconditioned on observations) generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\\dt > 1$ (Stage~1 above, $1.68\\times$ ratio under random policy), and the critic accumulates these into overestimated returns. The one-step reward prediction is negatively biased when conditioned on real observations, but this posterior correction is unavailable during imagination. This also distinguishes CIVO from generic compounding error, which would produce \\emph{positive} signed error accumulating with horizon; the negative observation-level bias confirms that CIVO is a prior-drift phenomenon, not a reward-prediction artifact.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Signed reward error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions. Negative values indicate underprediction. The trained-embedding model underpredicts at $\\dt{=}2$, ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the CIVO mechanism.}\n\\label{tab:signed_error}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & $\\dt$ & \\textbf{Mean SE} & \\textbf{Std} & \\textbf{\\%Over} & \\textbf{Actual} & \\textbf{Pred} \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline & 1 & $+0.001$ & 0.023 & 39.6\\% & 1.893 & 1.894 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008d (trained) & 1 & $+0.018$ & 0.072 & 61.8\\% & 0.361 & 0.379 \\\\\n008d (trained) & 2 & $\\mathbf{-0.599}$ & 0.722 & 8.0\\% & 0.721 & 0.121 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008f (no emb) & 1 & $-0.710$ & 0.704 & 14.0\\% & 0.921 & 0.211 \\\\\n008f (no emb) & 2 & $-1.645$ & 1.485 & 5.0\\% & 1.840 & 0.195 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Selective Gradient Stopping and Gradient Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}\n\n\\subsubsection{Gradient Decomposition Results}\n\\label{sec:gradient_decomposition}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_gradient_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Gradient contribution to $e_{\\Delta t}$ by loss component at two training checkpoints. The reward head contributes the largest share (38--49\\%) yet reward-only SGS (exp\\_016e) fails; the observation decoder channel (16--22\\%) carries the critical $\\dt$-specific spatial information that drives the RSSM fidelity gap.}\n\\label{fig:gradient_decomp}\n\\end{figure}\n\nExp\\_016e collapses in the same qualitative pattern as the unmodified control: peak evaluation 326.7 at 75K steps followed by catastrophic decline to 31.7 at 95K, with NVE reaching $10.42\\times$. The SGS intervention neither prevented nor attenuated CIVO.\n\nTo explain this null result, we performed a gradient decomposition analysis using \\texttt{torch.autograd.grad} to measure each loss component's contribution to $\\nabla_\\theta e_{\\Delta t}$, where $\\theta$ denotes the temporal embedding parameters. Table~\\ref{tab:gradient_decomposition} reports the results.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Gradient contributions to the temporal embedding $e_{\\Delta t}$ by loss component, measured via \\texttt{torch.autograd.grad} with training configuration \\texttt{gate\\_fixed\\_dt=2}. Gradient norms are $\\ell_2$ norms of $\\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{L}_i$ for each loss $\\mathcal{L}_i$.}\n\\label{tab:gradient_decomposition}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{@55K}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{@112K}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\nLoss component & Share & (\\%) & Share & (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nReward head & --- & 49.0 & --- & 38.1 \\\\\nKL dynamics & --- & 34.7 & --- & 40.4 \\\\\nObservation decoders & --- & 16.3 & --- & 21.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe gradient decomposition reveals a counterintuitive structure: the reward head provides the largest gradient contribution (49.0\\% at 55K, 38.1\\% at 112K), yet reward-only SGS (exp\\_016e) fails catastrophically (NVE $10.42\\times$). The observation decoder channel contributes the smallest share (16.3\\% at 55K, 21.5\\% at 112K) yet carries the critical $\\dt$-specific spatial information.  This dissociation between gradient magnitude and causal importance explains why exp\\_016e, which targets the largest pathway, is functionally insufficient for CIVO prevention.\n\nThe gradient structure is relatively stable across training (reward shifts from 49\\% to 38\\%, obs from 16\\% to 22\\%, KL from 35\\% to 40\\%), unlike what a magnitude-based account would predict. The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects the scalar reward prediction task's sensitivity to temporal embedding parameters, but this gradient does not encode $\\Delta t$-specific structural information about spatial dynamics. The observation decoder channel, despite its smaller magnitude, is the only pathway that encodes how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales.\n\n\\paragraph{Why the smallest channel is causally critical.} The observation decoder is the only loss component whose gradients encode how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states ($14$ orientations, $9$ velocities, $1$ height) across temporal scales.  This $\\dt$-specific spatial information shapes the latent state representation in ways that propagate to reward prediction: the RSSM fidelity gap ($2.78\\times$ reward MAE ratio, Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) arises because the temporal embedding learns to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\\dt{>}1$, not because the reward head directly learns scale-dependent biases.  Full-SGS targets this causal chain by preventing observation-decoder gradients from updating $e_{\\Delta t}$, though its 0/2 CIVO elimination rate on Walker (seed~1: NVE reduced from $4.12\\times$ to $1.66\\times$ but remains CIVO-positive; seed~2: collapsed) indicates that breaking the gradient pathway alone does not fully eliminate the vulnerability.\n\n\\subsubsection{Implications for the CIVO Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:sgs_implications}\n\nThe gradient decomposition reveals that gradient magnitude and causal importance are decoupled in CIVO.\n\n\\paragraph{Gradient importance $\\neq$ gradient magnitude.} The reward head contributes the largest gradient share (38--49\\%) to $e_{\\Delta t}$, yet reward-only SGS fails.  The observation decoder contributes the smallest share (16--22\\%), yet its removal (via Full-SGS) is necessary for CIVO mitigation.  This dissociation arises because the reward head's gradient, while large, does not encode $\\dt$-specific structural information: it adjusts $e_{\\Delta t}$ to minimize a scalar prediction error that is largely $\\dt$-invariant.  The observation decoder's gradient, though smaller, encodes how the RSSM should differentially reconstruct 24-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales. This is the channel through which $e_{\\Delta t}$ learns scale-dependent latent biases.\n\n\\paragraph{Reframing the intervention target.} Any gradient-level strategy that targets the reward head alone (reward prediction regularization, reward-conditioned value clipping, or reward-only SGS) targets the largest pathway but not the informationally critical one.  The failure of exp\\_016e (NVE $10.42\\times$) despite detaching the dominant gradient source confirms this.  Full-pathway SGS (exp\\_018) detaches $e_{\\Delta t}$ from all non-KL gradients, retaining only the KL dynamics contribution (34--40\\%).  However, this intervention eliminates CIVO in neither seed: seed~1 shows partial mitigation (eval 448.3, peak NVE $1.66\\times$, still CIVO-positive) while seed~2 collapses (eval 40.8). Removing the observation decoder gradient channel is thus contributory but not sufficient. Figure~\\ref{fig:civo_decomposition} decomposes evaluation returns across conditions.\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_civo_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{CIVO decomposition: peak and final (105K step) evaluation returns on Walker Walk across four conditions. \\textbf{Baseline} (001): vanilla DreamerV3. \\textbf{Trained dt-emb} (008d)\\protect\\footnotemark: learned $d{=}32$ temporal embedding with multi-rate data. \\textbf{Full-SGS} (s1, s2): trained embedding with all non-KL gradients detached from $e_{\\dt}$. Trained $\\dt$-embedding degrades peak eval by $73\\%$ and final eval by $86\\%$. Full-SGS seed~1 shows partial mitigation ($-51\\%$ final, NVE $1.66\\times$, still CIVO-positive) while seed~2 collapses ($-96\\%$ final). CIVO elimination rate $= 0/2$.}\n\\label{fig:civo_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\\footnotetext{Experiment 008d terminated at 55K steps; the 105K data point uses continuation run 009e under identical configuration.}\n\nCross-environment validation on Cheetah Run (exp\\_021) further clarifies the role of Full-SGS.  The intervention did not prevent the initial CIVO onset: NVE peaked at $9.94\\times$ at 45K (comparable to the unmitigated exp\\_010b's $8.27\\times$).  However, unlike exp\\_010b, which collapsed permanently to eval 51.7, exp\\_021 self-corrected within ${\\sim}$10K steps, with sustained NVE dropping to $0.34\\times$ (55K--105K) and final evaluation recovering to 221.5.  This transient self-correcting trajectory is not attributable to the SGS intervention: the initial CIVO onset and peak severity are comparable to the unmitigated case, and the self-correction may reflect Cheetah Run's simpler dynamics rather than SGS efficacy.  Performance remains at 38.7\\% of baseline (572.5), likely because Full-SGS over-aggressively blocks actor gradients (actor gradient norms near zero throughout training).  Combined with exp\\_018's Walker results (0/2 CIVO elimination), these findings yield a 0/3 elimination rate across environments, indicating that gradient-level interventions alone do not eliminate the CIVO vulnerability. Additional mechanisms (e.g., forward-pass conditioning effects, residual capacity-dependent instabilities) operate beyond the gradient pathway.\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to RSSM fidelity analysis.} The trained-embedding condition (exp\\_008d) shows a $2.78\\times$ reward gap between $\\Delta t{=}1$ and $\\Delta t{=}2$ transitions (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}).  This gap arises because the observation decoder gradients shape $e_{\\Delta t}$ to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\\dt{>}1$, and the reward head inherits these biases \\textit{through the latent state} rather than learning them directly.  The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects sensitivity to $e_{\\Delta t}$'s parameters but not causal responsibility for the fidelity gap.\n\n\\subsubsection{Entropy Dynamics Across Configurations}\n\\label{sec:entropy_dynamics}\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_entropy_dynamics.pdf}\n\\caption{Actor entropy trajectories for three configurations. All start at ${\\sim}8$ nats and converge to similar values (${\\sim}{-}4.5$ to ${-}5$). The CIVO-triggering configuration (008d, red) follows a normal entropy reduction trajectory; the divergence from the control (008f) is subtle in entropy space, consistent with CIVO operating through the value channel rather than the entropy channel. 018v2 (Full-SGS, seed~1) shows slightly lower terminal entropy.}\n\\label{fig:entropy_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsubsection{Exp\\_016e Reinterpreted as Stochastic Variation}\n\\label{sec:016e_reinterp}\n\nThe gradient decomposition establishes that exp\\_016e targets the largest gradient pathway (reward: 38--49\\%) but not the informationally critical one (observation decoders: 16--22\\%).  The apparent timing differences between exp\\_016e and exp\\_008d (later collapse onset, 75K vs.\\ 25K, and higher peak evaluation, 326.7 vs.\\ 167.2) must be attributed to stochastic variation rather than SGS effects, since the critical observation decoder channel remains intact.  All three trajectories (exp\\_008d, exp\\_009b, exp\\_016e) share the invariant identified by the NVE diagnostic: each crosses the $1.5\\times$ pathological threshold at some point during training.\n\n\\subsection{Post-CIVO Outcomes: Why Seeds Diverge}\n\\label{sec:seed_divergence}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Post-CIVO outcomes for all seven CIVO-positive seeds across two environments. Peak/Final NVE measured in the 35K+ window.}\n\\label{tab:post_civo}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccl}",

617	All seven CIVO-positive seeds cross the $\nve > 1.5\times$ threshold (Table~\ref{tab:post_civo}), yet the downstream damage is stochastic.\footnote{Exp\_009e was excluded from multi-seed analyses due to a seed configuration error (default seed=0, identical to exp\_008d).}  Full collapse is the majority outcome (6/7 seeds), with one self-correction case (Walker seed 2); seed 5 exemplifies a dissociation where NVE self-corrects ($4.72\times \to 1.22\times$) while the policy remains collapsed (eval 10.5\% of baseline). We hypothesize that the divergence relates to the critic's representational capacity at the time of CIVO onset.  \citet{lyle2022capacity} demonstrate that deep RL networks suffer progressive capacity loss, in which early-learned features become increasingly difficult to overwrite.  If the critic's capacity is already partially consumed when CIVO injects overestimation, the network cannot recalibrate (seed~0); if sufficient capacity remains, the critic can absorb transient overestimation and recover (seed~2).
