633	\toprule
634	\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{Representative} & \textbf{NVE} & \textbf{Entropy} & \textbf{Mechanism} \\
635	\midrule
636	Monotonic CIVO & 008d, 008d\_s4, 010b\_s0 & $\uparrow$ sustained & $\downarrow$ & Reward bias $\to$ critic $\to$ actor \\
637	Late-onset CIVO & 008d\_s3 & $\uparrow$ delayed & $\downarrow$ & Same, delayed onset \\
638	Transient CIVO & 009b & Peak $\to$ $\downarrow$ & Stable & Self-correction via capacity \\
639	IRD collapse & 008e\_v3\_s2 & $\uparrow$ late & $\uparrow$ & Entropy-driven, values stable \\
640	Grad.\ starvation & 015b & Oscillates, decays & Stable & Frozen emb $\to$ zero actor grad \\
641	SGS-resistant & 018\_s2 & $\uparrow$ & $\downarrow$ & Forward-pass conditioning \\
642	SGS-paradoxical & 016e & $\uparrow$ sustained & $\downarrow$ & Reward SGS $\to$ obs-dec reliance \\
643	\bottomrule
644	\end{tabular}
645	\end{table}
646	
647	\subsection{Extended Training Dynamics}\label{sec:extended_training}
648	
649	One Walker seed (s3, exp\_045) was extended from 112K to approximately 302K steps to investigate long-horizon CIVO dynamics. After onset (NVE peak $4.91\times$ at 132K steps), the system exhibited complete self-recovery: NVE declined to $0.05\times$ and evaluation performance recovered to 867.6 (94.7\% of baseline). This recovery required approximately 170K additional training steps beyond onset. The trajectory shows a single onset--recovery cycle with no recurrence, suggesting that the CIVO feedback loop can break spontaneously given sufficient training---though at substantial computational cost that the frozen $\dt$-embedding diagnostic eliminates entirely.
650	
651	\subsection{Why MPC Is Immune}
652	\label{sec:mpc_immune}
653	
654	\citet{nhu2025tawm} show that $\dt$-conditioning benefits TD-MPC2, raising the question of why it fails in DreamerV3.  The critical difference is that MPC re-plans from real states at every step, so imagination errors do not accumulate into the training signal because there is no critic bootstrapping from imagined trajectories.  In DreamerV3, the actor-critic trains entirely within imagination, and any systematic reward-prediction bias at $\dt > 1$ is amplified through the closed actor-critic loop.
655	
656	\subsection{The \texorpdfstring{$\dt$}{dt}-Embedding Dilemma}
657	\label{sec:embedding_dilemma}
658	
659	The factorial results reveal a fundamental tension: removing the $\dt$-embedding (008f) eliminates CIVO but at a $13\times$ cost to reward prediction quality ($0.7469$ vs.\ $0.0568$ MAE), as the world model cannot distinguish temporal scales.  Restricting imagination to $\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding (008e\_v3) avoids collapse in 2 of 4 seeds (50\%), while the remaining two collapse via distinct mechanisms: Pathway~A (CIVO-driven, entropy decrease; s3, NVE $4.02\times$) and Pathway~B (entropy-driven, entropy increase; s2, NVE $3.78\times$; \S\ref{sec:f6}).  Neither approach alone provides a complete solution.
660	
661	\subsection{Multiple Vulnerability Pathways}
662	\label{sec:pathways}
663	
664	The two collapse patterns identified in \S\ref{sec:f6}---CIVO (Pathway~A, value-driven, entropy decreasing) and a tentative second mode (Pathway~B, eval-driven, entropy increasing)---have distinct diagnostic signatures. Pathway~B is observed in only two seeds (008e\_v3\_s2 and 038\_s2); we present it as preliminary evidence that the trained $\dt$-embedding's learned features may affect both reward prediction across temporal scales (Pathway~A) and the representation space in which the actor operates (Pathway~B), though additional data is needed to confirm this as a distinct mechanism.  This explains why $\nve$ is sensitive but not perfectly specific: Pathway~B's late evaluation decline inflates $\nve$ through the same arithmetic as CIVO, yielding the sole false positive ($3.78\times$ for 008e\_v3 s2).  Distinguishing the two requires examining the temporal profile and entropy direction of collapse (Figure~\ref{fig:multiple_pathways}).
665	
666	\begin{figure}[ht]
667	\centering
668	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig6_multiple_pathways.pdf}
669	\caption{Contrasting collapse dynamics. \textbf{Left:} 008d (CIVO), value explosion with entropy decrease. \textbf{Right:} 008e\_v3 s2 (non-CIVO), evaluation crash with entropy increase.}
670	\label{fig:multiple_pathways}
671	\end{figure}
672	
673	\subsection{Implications for Temporal Abstraction in World Models}
674	\label{sec:implications}
675	
676	CIVO identifies a structural incompatibility between flat temporal conditioning and imagination-based actor-critic training.  Hierarchical approaches (THICK \citep{gumbsch2024thick}, Hieros \citep{mattes2024hieros}, LS-Imagine \citep{li2025lsimagine}) may avoid this by maintaining separate dynamics per temporal scale.  Whether CIVO transfers to transformer-based world models (DreamerV4; \citealt{hafner2025dreamerv4}) depends on whether attention weights co-adapt with temporal embeddings analogously.  The CIVO mechanism requires three structural properties: (1) a temporal embedding that co-adapts with dynamics via gradient flow, (2) multi-step imagined rollouts used for critic training, and (3) a reward head that inherits latent biases from the shared representation.  Transformer-based world models that share these properties may exhibit analogous vulnerability; architectures that decouple temporal conditioning from the reward prediction pathway would not.
677	
678	\subsection{Cross-Environment TCN Evaluation}
679	\label{sec:tcn_cross_env}
680	
681	Table~\ref{tab:tcn_full} reports the full TCN evaluation across environments and noise magnitudes.
682	
683	\begin{table}[h]
684	\centering
685	\caption{TCN results across environments and noise magnitudes. Prevention = peak NVE $< 1.5\times$ (35K+).}
686	\label{tab:tcn_full}
687	\footnotesize
688	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
689	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
690	\toprule
691	\textbf{Env} & $\sigma$ & \textbf{Seed} & \textbf{NVE$_{\max}$} & \textbf{Prevented?} & \textbf{Eval (\%)} \\
692	\midrule
693	Walker & 0.1 & s0 & 1.02 & \checkmark$^*$ & 55\% \\
694	Walker & 0.2 & s0 & 0.91 & \checkmark & 91\% \\
695	Walker & 0.2 & s1 & 0.64 & \checkmark & 45\% \\
696	Walker & 0.2 & s2 & 2.11$^\dagger$ & $\times$ & ${\sim}5\%$ \\
697	\rowcolor{gray!15}
698	Walker & 0.2 & s3 & 3.04$^\P$ & $\times$ & 11\% \\
699	Walker & 0.5 & s0 & 3.97 & $\times$ & 27\% \\
700	Walker & 0.5 & s1 & 1.64 & $\times$ & 41\% \\
701	\midrule
702	Cheetah & 0.2 & s0 & 1.90 & $\times$ & crashed \\
703	Cheetah & 0.5 & s0 & 10.67 & $\times$ & 26\% \\
704	Cheetah & 1.0 & s0 & $\leq$0.30 & \checkmark$^\S$ & 15\% \\
705	\midrule
706	PickCube & 0.2 & s0 & 3.71 & $\times$ & failing \\
707	\bottomrule
708	\end{tabular}
709	\vspace{1pt}
710	{\raggedright\footnotesize $^*$Borderline: NVE${=}1.02\times$ at 95K, below $1.5\times$ threshold but above $1.0\times$.\\
711	$^\dagger$NVE $= 2.11\times$ at 95K, still rising; eval ${\sim}5\%$ of baseline.\\
712	$^\P$Late-onset CIVO: NVE declined to $0.67\times$ at 95K before phase-transition-like spike to $3.04\times$ at 105K; eval 349.6 at 95K $\to$ 71.8 at 105K $\to$ 104.5 at 215K.\\
713	$^\S$CIVO prevented (NVE${\leq}0.30$); peak eval 116\% baseline at 35K, declining to 15\% by 215K via progressive policy erosion. See ``impossible dose'' discussion.\par}
714	\end{table}
715	
716	\paragraph{Cheetah Run $\sigma{=}0.5$: transient spike with permanent eval damage.}
717	Cheetah $\sigma{=}0.5$ exhibits a novel failure mode: a transient CIVO spike (NVE $= 10.67\times$ at 55K) followed by NVE recovery ($< 0.3\times$ by 95K), but permanent evaluation damage (26\% of baseline). The value signal self-corrects while the policy does not recover, suggesting that CIVO-induced representational damage during the spike persists within the observation window even when the critic subsequently recalibrates. This trajectory parallels the obs-decoder SGS seed~3 pattern (\S\ref{sec:seed_divergence}), where partial policy recovery coexists with persistent critic damage.
718	
719	\paragraph{Cheetah Run $\sigma{=}0.2$: insufficient dose.}
720	At $\sigma{=}0.2$, Cheetah reaches NVE $= 1.9\times$ at 55K---exceeding the $1.5\times$ threshold---consistent with Cheetah's higher baseline CIVO severity ($8$--$108\times$ vs.\ Walker's $2$--$6\times$). The noise magnitude that suffices for Walker is inadequate for Cheetah, but increasing $\sigma$ to 0.5 trades CIVO prevention for a different failure mode rather than achieving mitigation.
721	
722	\paragraph{PickCube-v1 $\sigma{=}0.2$: manipulation failure.}
723	PickCube reaches NVE $= 3.71\times$ at 52.5K with $\sigma{=}0.2$, indicating that TCN provides no protection in manipulation tasks. The combination of contact-gated dynamics and temporal conditioning may amplify the fidelity gap beyond what representation-level noise can attenuate.
724	
725	\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}
726	We observe $\sigma{=}0.2$ as a local optimum on Walker Walk, achieving complete CIVO suppression in 2 of 4 seeds. Seeds~2 and~3 both fail but via distinct CIVO onset dynamics: seed~2 shows gradual escalation (NVE $= 2.11\times$ at 95K, still rising, eval ${\sim}5\%$), while seed~3 exhibits a phase-transition-like onset---NVE declined to $0.67\times$ at 95K before spiking to $3.04\times$ at 105K (eval 349.6 $\to$ 71.8 in 10K steps). When TCN fails to prevent CIVO onset, the resulting trajectory qualitatively resembles untreated CIVO in its terminal outcome---runaway NVE and eval collapse. The two failure modes (gradual escalation vs.\ sudden onset after apparent suppression) reinforce that TCN operates as a binary gate on CIVO onset rather than providing graded attenuation. Below the optimum, we observe $\sigma{=}0.1$ as insufficient (NVE $= 1.02\times$---borderline, eval 55\%); above it, $\sigma{=}0.5$ overdoses (oscillatory CIVO, eval $\leq 41\%$). Cross-seed variance at $\sigma{=}0.2$ (eval 5--91\%) exceeds the cross-dose difference by a wide margin, reinforcing that seed effects dominate dose effects. TCN is the only tested intervention achieving complete CIVO suppression in any configuration, but its high seed sensitivity and failure to transfer across environments position it as a \emph{diagnostic probe}---identifying the representation level as the relevant intervention target---rather than a deployable mitigation.
727	
728	\paragraph{Cheetah Run ``impossible dose'' dilemma.}
729	Exp\_030c (224K steps) reveals a qualitatively different dose-response on Cheetah. At $\sigma{=}1.0$, CIVO is eliminated (NVE${\leq}0.30$ throughout) and initial learning is \emph{unimpaired}: peak eval reaches 372 at 35K (116\% of baseline), demonstrating that $\sigma{=}1.0$ noise does not prevent policy acquisition. However, cumulative actor gradient interference produces progressive policy erosion---eval declines from 372 to 47 by 215K (15\% of baseline)---while the critic remains well-calibrated (value\_mean${=}12.4$, NVE${=}0.26$ at 215K). This establishes that \emph{policy degradation $\neq$ value corruption}: accurate value signals are insufficient when the actor cannot optimize effectively in noise-perturbed imagination.
730	
731	This ``correct values, failed policy'' pattern provides indirect evidence that CIVO is mechanistically distinct from standard overestimation. Double Q-learning and ensemble methods address critic estimation error, but at $\sigma{=}1.0$ the critic is accurate and the policy still collapses---a failure mode orthogonal to what these countermeasures target. The impossible dose dilemma scales with environment complexity: Cheetah's baseline CIVO severity ($8$--$108\times$ NVE) requires $\sigma \geq 1.0$ to suppress, but this exceeds the actor's gradient signal-to-noise tolerance. On Walker, a narrow effective window exists around $\sigma{=}0.2$; on Cheetah, the effective dose ($\sigma \geq 1.0$) exceeds the tolerable dose, and environments with higher CIVO severity will systematically lack a viable dose window.
732

Transformer-based world models that share these properties may exhibit analogous vulnerability; architectures that decouple temporal conditioning from the reward prediction pathway would not.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment TCN Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:tcn_cross_env}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:tcn_full} reports the full TCN evaluation across environments and noise magnitudes.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{TCN results across environments and noise magnitudes. Prevention = peak NVE $< 1.5\\times$ (35K+).}\n\\label{tab:tcn_full}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Env} & $\\sigma$ & \\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{NVE$_{\\max}$} & \\textbf{Prevented?} & \\textbf{Eval (\\%)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nWalker & 0.1 & s0 & 1.02 & \\checkmark$^*$ & 55\\% \\\\\nWalker & 0.2 & s0 & 0.91 & \\checkmark & 91\\% \\\\\nWalker & 0.2 & s1 & 0.64 & \\checkmark & 45\\% \\\\\nWalker & 0.2 & s2 & 2.11$^\\dagger$ & $\\times$ & ${\\sim}5\\%$ \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\nWalker & 0.2 & s3 & 3.04$^\\P$ & $\\times$ & 11\\% \\\\\nWalker & 0.5 & s0 & 3.97 & $\\times$ & 27\\% \\\\\nWalker & 0.5 & s1 & 1.64 & $\\times$ & 41\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nCheetah & 0.2 & s0 & 1.90 & $\\times$ & crashed \\\\\nCheetah & 0.5 & s0 & 10.67 & $\\times$ & 26\\% \\\\\nCheetah & 1.0 & s0 & $\\leq$0.30 & \\checkmark$^\\S$ & 15\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nPickCube & 0.2 & s0 & 3.71 & $\\times$ & failing \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^*$Borderline: NVE${=}1.02\\times$ at 95K, below $1.5\\times$ threshold but above $1.0\\times$.\\\\\n$^\\dagger$NVE $= 2.11\\times$ at 95K, still rising; eval ${\\sim}5\\%$ of baseline.\\\\\n$^\\P$Late-onset CIVO: NVE declined to $0.67\\times$ at 95K before phase-transition-like spike to $3.04\\times$ at 105K; eval 349.6 at 95K $\\to$ 71.8 at 105K $\\to$ 104.5 at 215K.\\\\\n$^\\S$CIVO prevented (NVE${\\leq}0.30$); peak eval 116\\% baseline at 35K, declining to 15\\% by 215K via progressive policy erosion. See ``impossible dose'' discussion.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Cheetah Run $\\sigma{=}0.5$: transient spike with permanent eval damage.}\nCheetah $\\sigma{=}0.5$ exhibits a novel failure mode: a transient CIVO spike (NVE $= 10.67\\times$ at 55K) followed by NVE recovery ($< 0.3\\times$ by 95K), but permanent evaluation damage (26\\% of baseline). The value signal self-corrects while the policy does not recover, suggesting that CIVO-induced representational damage during the spike persists within the observation window even when the critic subsequently recalibrates. This trajectory parallels the obs-decoder SGS seed~3 pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:seed_divergence}), where partial policy recovery coexists with persistent critic damage.\n\n\\paragraph{Cheetah Run $\\sigma{=}0.2$: insufficient dose.}\nAt $\\sigma{=}0.2$, Cheetah reaches NVE $= 1.9\\times$ at 55K---exceeding the $1.5\\times$ threshold---consistent with Cheetah's higher baseline CIVO severity ($8$--$108\\times$ vs.\\ Walker's $2$--$6\\times$). The noise magnitude that suffices for Walker is inadequate for Cheetah, but increasing $\\sigma$ to 0.5 trades CIVO prevention for a different failure mode rather than achieving mitigation.\n\n\\paragraph{PickCube-v1 $\\sigma{=}0.2$: manipulation failure.}\nPickCube reaches NVE $= 3.71\\times$ at 52.5K with $\\sigma{=}0.2$, indicating that TCN provides no protection in manipulation tasks. The combination of contact-gated dynamics and temporal conditioning may amplify the fidelity gap beyond what representation-level noise can attenuate.\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}\nWe observe $\\sigma{=}0.2$ as a local optimum on Walker Walk, achieving complete CIVO suppression in 2 of 4 seeds. Seeds~2 and~3 both fail but via distinct CIVO onset dynamics: seed~2 shows gradual escalation (NVE $= 2.11\\times$ at 95K, still rising, eval ${\\sim}5\\%$), while seed~3 exhibits a phase-transition-like onset---NVE declined to $0.67\\times$ at 95K before spiking to $3.04\\times$ at 105K (eval 349.6 $\\to$ 71.8 in 10K steps). When TCN fails to prevent CIVO onset, the resulting trajectory qualitatively resembles untreated CIVO in its terminal outcome---runaway NVE and eval collapse. The two failure modes (gradual escalation vs.\\ sudden onset after apparent suppression) reinforce that TCN operates as a binary gate on CIVO onset rather than providing graded attenuation. Below the optimum, we observe $\\sigma{=}0.1$ as insufficient (NVE $= 1.02\\times$---borderline, eval 55\\%); above it, $\\sigma{=}0.5$ overdoses (oscillatory CIVO, eval $\\leq 41\\%$). Cross-seed variance at $\\sigma{=}0.2$ (eval 5--91\\%) exceeds the cross-dose difference by a wide margin, reinforcing that seed effects dominate dose effects. TCN is the only tested intervention achieving complete CIVO suppression in any configuration, but its high seed sensitivity and failure to transfer across environments position it as a \\emph{diagnostic probe}---identifying the representation level as the relevant intervention target---rather than a deployable mitigation.\n\n\\paragraph{Cheetah Run ``impossible dose'' dilemma.}\nExp\\_030c (224K steps) reveals a qualitatively different dose-response on Cheetah. At $\\sigma{=}1.0$, CIVO is eliminated (NVE${\\leq}0.30$ throughout) and initial learning is \\emph{unimpaired}: peak eval reaches 372 at 35K (116\\% of baseline), demonstrating that $\\sigma{=}1.0$ noise does not prevent policy acquisition. However, cumulative actor gradient interference produces progressive policy erosion---eval declines from 372 to 47 by 215K (15\\% of baseline)---while the critic remains well-calibrated (value\\_mean${=}12.4$, NVE${=}0.26$ at 215K). This establishes that \\emph{policy degradation $\\neq$ value corruption}: accurate value signals are insufficient when the actor cannot optimize effectively in noise-perturbed imagination.\n\nThis ``correct values, failed policy'' pattern provides indirect evidence that CIVO is mechanistically distinct from standard overestimation. Double Q-learning and ensemble methods address critic estimation error, but at $\\sigma{=}1.0$ the critic is accurate and the policy still collapses---a failure mode orthogonal to what these countermeasures target. The impossible dose dilemma scales with environment complexity: Cheetah's baseline CIVO severity ($8$--$108\\times$ NVE) requires $\\sigma \\geq 1.0$ to suppress, but this exceeds the actor's gradient signal-to-noise tolerance. On Walker, a narrow effective window exists around $\\sigma{=}0.2$; on Cheetah, the effective dose ($\\sigma \\geq 1.0$) exceeds the tolerable dose, and environments with higher CIVO severity will systematically lack a viable dose window.\n\n\\section{DreamerV3 Hyperparameters}\n\\label{sec:hyperparameters}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:hyperparams} lists the DreamerV3 hyperparameters used across all experiments. Unless otherwise noted, we use the default DreamerV3 configuration for continuous-control tasks (\\texttt{dmc\\_proprio}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DreamerV3 hyperparameters for all experiments. Modifications from defaults are marked with $\\diamond$.}\n\\label{tab:hyperparams}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{ll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Parameter} & \\textbf{Value} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{World Model}} \\\\\nRSSM discrete states & 32 classes $\\times$ 32 categories \\\\\nRSSM stochastic dim & 32 \\\\\nRSSM deterministic dim & 512 \\\\\nMLP hidden size & 512 \\\\\nMLP layers & 2 \\\\\nActivation & SiLU \\\\\nNormalization & LayerNorm \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{Training}} \\\\\nBatch size & 16 sequences $\\times$ 64 steps \\\\\nLearning rate & $3 \\times 10^{-4}$ (Adam, $\\epsilon{=}10^{-8}$) \\\\\nSequence length & 64 \\\\\nTraining ratio & 512 \\\\\nFree nats & 1.0 \\\\\nKL balance ($\\alpha$) & 0.8 \\\\\nDiscount ($\\gamma$) & 0.997 \\\\\n$\\lambda$-return & 0.95 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{Policy}} \\\\\nImagination horizon$^\\diamond$ & 8 or 15 (see Table~\\ref{tab:complete_results}) \\\\\nActor entropy scale & $3 \\times 10^{-4}$ \\\\\nActor gradient & Reinforce + dynamics backprop \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{Temporal Abstraction}$^\\diamond$} \\\\\nGate enabled & True (all non-baseline) \\\\\nGate fixed $\\dt$ & 1 or 2 (per config) \\\\\nSubsample max $\\dt$ & 1 (OFF) or 2 (ON) \\\\\n$\\dt$-embedding dim & 32 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{Environment}} \\\\\nAction repeat & 2 (Walker Walk, Cheetah Run) \\\\\nObservation & Proprioceptive (no pixels) \\\\\nTotal environment steps & 105K \\\\\nEvaluation frequency & Every 10K steps \\\\\nEvaluation episodes & 10 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Online CIVO Detector Details}\n\\label{sec:detector_appendix}\n\nThis appendix provides the full algorithm specification and validation results for the online CIVO detector described in \\S\\ref{sec:detector}.\n\n\\subsection{Algorithm Specification}\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[ht]\n\\caption{Online CIVO Detector}\n\\label{alg:civo_detector}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Stream of 70	\subsection{F2: Trained Temporal Embedding Precedes Value Overestimation}
71	\label{sec:f2}
72	
73	The factorial design isolates trained $\dt$-embedding as the strongest predictor. The \emph{primary} comparison---008d (trained, subsample ON) vs.\ 008f (no embedding, subsample ON)---differs only in embedding training, yielding $\nve = 4.12\times$ (eval 82.8) vs.\ $\nve \leq 0.97\times$ (eval 503.0). \emph{Secondary} comparisons against 008a/008c (unexposed embedding) co-vary subsample status and horizon alongside the embedding factor. The $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold (35K+ window) correctly classifies 27 of 30 experimental configurations across two environments (AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}; Figure~\ref{fig:nve_trajectory}).
74	\begin{figure}[t]
75	\centering
76	\includegraphics[width=0.65\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_nve_trajectory.pdf}
77	\caption{NVE trajectories (mean $\pm$ seed range). Trained $\dt$-embedding (red, Walker Walk, $n{=}3$ seeds plotted; seed~4 data in Table~\ref{tab:complete_results}) exceeds the $1.5\times$ threshold (dashed) in all seeds; no-embedding control (blue) remains near $1.0\times$. Three trajectory types emerge: early collapse (seed~0), late-onset collapse (seed~3), and self-correction (seed~2). Dotted line marks the 35K diagnostic window onset.}
78	\label{fig:nve_trajectory}
79	\end{figure}
80	
81	The trained $\dt$-embedding precedes CIVO in all five Walker Walk seeds (peak NVE $1.53$--$4.72\times$), universally degrading performance to below 30\% of baseline (IQM $175.2$, $n{=}5$; \citealt{agarwal2021precipice}). Combined with Cheetah Run (\S\ref{sec:f6}), CIVO onset is universal across all seven tested seeds ($n{=}7$, two environments) with 6/7 suffering sustained policy collapse, 1/7 self-correcting (Appendix~\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).
82	
83	%% ============================================================
84	%% F3--F5: Summary (details in Appendix B)
85	%% ============================================================
86	
87	\subsection{F3--F5: Factorial Ablation (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial})}
88	\label{sec:f3f5_summary}
89	
90	Trained $\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure are \emph{jointly sufficient in tested configurations} for CIVO. Neither factor alone precedes pathological overestimation: trained embedding without multi-rate exposure (008a, 3 seeds, peak NVE $0.83$--$0.93\times$) and multi-rate exposure without embedding (008f, 3 seeds, peak NVE $\leq 1.41\times$) both remain below $1.5\times$. Cross-environment replication on Cheetah Run confirms generalization. Freezing the embedding (\texttt{requires\_grad=False}) dramatically reduces severity (NVE $0.19$--$1.88\times$ vs.\ trained $1.53$--$4.72\times$; 2/4 seeds below threshold, 2/4 transient self-correcting spikes; Table~\ref{tab:three_way_emb}); removing the embedding (008f) also avoids CIVO. Partial gradient blocking (exp\_044) does not prevent onset (peak NVE $4.45\times$) but permits delayed self-correction (final eval 538, 59\% baseline; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}), confirming that \emph{any} gradient pathway updating $e_{\dt}$ suffices to induce CIVO. Degradation scales monotonically with $\dt$ ($-47\%$ at $\dt{=}2$, $-81\%$ at $\dt{=}4$; Figure~\ref{fig:dt_amplification}).
91	
92	\paragraph{ManiSkill manipulation tasks.}
93	None of three ManiSkill environments exhibit $\dt$-embedding-dependent CIVO. All value-ratio spikes ($4$--$11\times$) are environment-specific: frozen controls show comparable or higher peaks (Table~\ref{tab:cross_domain}), and all self-correct. CIVO is locomotion-specific in DreamerV3; the frozen-embedding diagnostic correctly identifies these transients as non-CIVO.
94	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	World-model reinforcement learning enables sample-efficient learning directly on physical hardware \citep{wu2023daydreamer, hansen2024tdmpc2}. Real robotic tasks demand temporal abstraction: manipulation requires fast reactive grasping alongside slow approach planning, locomotion involves gait phases at different frequencies, and sim-to-real transfer must handle $\dt$ mismatches between simulation and hardware. TAWM \citep{nhu2025tawm} shows that conditioning a world model on step size $\dt$ yields consistent gains within TD-MPC2's MPC framework \citep{hansen2024tdmpc2}. Can the same $\dt$-conditioning bring temporal abstraction to imagination-based actor-critic methods like DreamerV3 \citep{hafner2023dreamerv3}, where policies are trained entirely in imagined rollouts?
5	
6	We find that it cannot. Every variable-rate configuration degrades performance catastrophically (14--19\% of baseline). Through a factorial analysis on Walker Walk \citep{tassa2018dmcontrol}, we isolate the combination of a \emph{trained} $\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor. This combination enables the RSSM reward head to develop a $2.78\times$ prediction gap between $\dt{=}1$ and $\dt{=}2$, which the critic internalizes as optimistic value estimates, a failure mode we term \emph{conditioning-induced value overestimation} (CIVO), analogous to delusional bias \citep{lu2018nondelusional} but model-induced rather than policy-class-induced. A normalized value-to-eval (NVE) threshold of $\geq 1.5\times$ achieves 27/30 classification ($n{=}30$, two environments, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, Appendix~Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}).
7	Gradient decomposition reveals that gradient magnitude does not predict causal importance: the reward head contributes 38--49\% of $\nabla_\theta e_{\dt}$ yet reward-only stopping fails, while the observation decoder (16--22\%) carries the critical signal. A four-level intervention hierarchy (gradient, output, representation, architecture) shows that no tested intervention reliably eliminates CIVO across environments. In pure-imagination training, dual critics converge to identical value functions ($\rho > 0.999$), rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective; the min operation nonetheless enables CIVO recovery, while prevention requires min $+$ increased capacity. These findings are directly relevant to robotics: as world-model RL moves toward real systems with variable-rate sensing and multi-timescale control, understanding when temporal conditioning fails, and why standard countermeasures cannot fix it, is essential for safe deployment.
8	
9	\paragraph{Contributions.}
10	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
11	    \item \textbf{Failure mode identification.} We identify CIVO as specific to imagination-based actor-critic with temporal conditioning and isolate trained $\dt$-embedding $+$ multi-rate exposure as jointly sufficient in tested configurations (7/7 seeds, two locomotion environments, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated). Cross-environment validation spanning locomotion and manipulation (ManiSkill) establishes that CIVO is locomotion-specific; frozen-embedding controls on three manipulation tasks identify value-ratio transients as environment-driven, demonstrating the diagnostic's specificity on non-CIVO dynamics.
12	    \item \textbf{Architectural boundary.} $\dt$-conditioning succeeds in TD-MPC2's MPC re-planning but fails under imagination-based bootstrapping, where model errors compound into biased value targets with no real-data anchor.
13	    \item \textbf{Four-level intervention hierarchy.} Across gradient, output, representation, and architecture levels, no intervention reliably eliminates CIVO across environments. Gradient magnitude does not predict causal importance (reward head: 38--49\%, reward-only stopping fails). In pure-imagination training, dual critics converge ($\rho > 0.999$), eliminating Double Q pessimism, though the min operation enables recovery through a different mechanism.
14	    \item \textbf{Preliminary diagnostic.} A proof-of-concept NVE-based detector achieves TPR 95\% CI $[47.8\%, 100\%]$ ($n{=}5/5$), FPR $22\%$ ($2/9$), with predictive warning in 2/5 cases.
15	\end{enumerate}
16	
```

```text
1	World-model reinforcement learning is increasingly deployed for robot control, where temporal abstraction enables planning across heterogeneous time scales. We find that $\Delta t$-conditioning, effective in model-predictive control (MPC), cannot transfer to imagination-based actor-critic training: every variable-rate DreamerV3 configuration degrades to 14--19\% of baseline. Factorial diagnostics on two continuous-control tasks identify trained temporal embedding with multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor of this failure---\emph{conditioning-induced value overestimation} (CIVO; 27/30, AUROC${=}0.953$, LOEO cross-validated). The RSSM develops a reward prediction gap at $\Delta t{>}1$ that the critic internalizes as optimistic values; the contrast with MPC identifies feedback architecture as a hypothesized structural factor. No tested intervention across four levels (gradient, output, representation, architecture) reliably eliminates CIVO: dual critics converge in pure imagination, rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective, though min $+$ increased capacity enables recovery.
2	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019V3QSAmFLGP7j7nKqo5mPX`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{CIVO mechanism.}
5	The RSSM develops a $2.78\times$ reward prediction gap under trained conditioning (Table~\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}), analogous to delusional bias \citep{lu2018nondelusional}. Gradient magnitude $\neq$ causal importance: the reward head (38--49\% of $\nabla_\theta e_{\dt}$) fails when blocked alone; the observation decoder (16--22\%) carries the critical signal; full-pathway SGS (0/3) confirms forward-pass conditioning as the irreducible vulnerability. CIVO is upstream of estimation error---dual critics converge (\S\ref{sec:arch_results})---so standard corrections are inapplicable; MPC re-planning prevents accumulation (Appendix~\ref{sec:mpc_immune}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Practitioner guidelines.}
8	Monitor via NVE diagnostic ($\geq 1.5\times$, AUROC${=}0.953$; Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}). Freezing the $\dt$-embedding converts CIVO onset (7/7 trained locomotion, NVE $1.53$--$108\times$) to transient spikes that self-correct within 20K steps (2/3 frozen Walker, $1.71$--$1.88\times$; 0/2 frozen Cheetah); untreated, extreme CIVO ($70.6\times$) causes irrecoverable damage (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_analysis}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Training horizon.}
11	Our primary analysis uses 105K steps, sufficient to observe CIVO onset (45K--65K) and classify trajectories. Extended training (${\sim}$302K steps, one seed) shows complete self-recovery after approximately 170K additional steps post-onset---at substantial computational cost that the frozen $\dt$-embedding diagnostic eliminates entirely (Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_training}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Implications for robot learning.}
14	No $\dt$-embedding-dependent CIVO was observed in manipulation (Table~\ref{tab:cross_domain}). Both domains use dense shaped rewards, so reward sparsity cannot explain the boundary. Possible mediators include action-space periodicity, observation structure, and continuous versus contact-gated dynamics; the precise factors remain an open question.
15	
```

</details>

1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
2	"""Recompute n=29 AUROC after fixing 008e_v3_s2 label from CIVO to non-CIVO (IRD)."""
3	
4	import numpy as np
5	import json
6	
7	# n=29 data from ROC curve analysis, with 008e_v3_s2 FIXED to non-CIVO
8	experiments = [
9	    # (exp_id, max_nve, true_label)  1=CIVO, 0=non-CIVO

12	    ("008a_unexp_s2",    0.333, 0),

19	    ("027_tcn_s0_σ02",   0.908, 0),
20	    ("008e_v3_s4",       0.990, 0),
21	    ("008f_s3",          1.000, 0),
22	    ("022b_nocurr",      1.220, 0),
23	    ("008f_s4",          1.410, 0),
24	    ("025_obs_sgs_s2",   1.730, 0),  # SGS failure, not CIVO
25	    ("028_rave_ceil15",  2.230, 0),  # anchor pollution, not CIVO
26	    ("008e_v3_s2",       3.780, 0),  # FIXED: was CIVO, now non-CIVO (IRD)
27	    ("008d_s3",          1.530, 1),
28	    ("027_tcn_s1",       1.637, 1),
29	    ("018v2_sgs_s1",     1.660, 1),
30	    ("019b",             3.780, 1),
31	    ("027_tcn_s0",       3.965, 1),
32	    ("008e_v3_s3",       4.020, 1),
33	    ("009e_s1",          4.120, 1),
34	    ("008d_s4",          4.620, 1),
35	    ("010b_s1",          8.270, 1),
36	    ("021_sgs_cheetah",  9.940, 1),
37	    ("016e_sgs_s1",     10.420, 1),
38	    ("010b_s0",         70.640, 1),
39	]
40	
41	ids = [e[0] for e in experiments]
42	scores = [e[1] for e in experiments]
43	labels = [e[2] for e in experiments]
44	
45	n_pos = sum(labels)
46	n_neg = len(labels) - n_pos
47	print(f"n={len(labels)}, positive={n_pos}, negative={n_neg}")
48	
49	
50	def compute_auroc(labs, scs):
51	    np_ = sum(labs)
52	    nn_ = len(labs) - np_
53	    if np_ == 0 or nn_ == 0:
54	        return float("nan")
55	    concordant = 0.0
56	    for i in range(len(labs)):
57	        if labs[i] == 1:
58	            for j in range(len(labs)):
59	                if labs[j] == 0:
60	                    if scs[i] > scs[j]:
61	                        concordant += 1.0
62	                    elif scs[i] == scs[j]:
63	                        concordant += 0.5
64	    return concordant / (np_ * nn_)
65	
66	
67	auroc = compute_auroc(labels, scores)
68	print(f"\nAUROC (corrected n=29): {auroc:.4f}")
69	print(f"Concordant pairs: {auroc * n_pos * n_neg:.1f} / {n_pos * n_neg}")
70	
71	# Bootstrap CI
72	rng = np.random.RandomState(42)
73	n_bootstrap = 10000
74	n = len(labels)
75	aurocs = []
76	for _ in range(n_bootstrap):
77	    idx = rng.choice(n, size=n, replace=True)
78	    bl = [labels[i] for i in idx]
79	    bs = [scores[i] for i in idx]
80	    if sum(bl) == 0 or sum(bl) == len(bl):
81	        continue
82	    a = compute_auroc(bl, bs)
83	    if not np.isnan(a):
84	        aurocs.append(a)
85	
86	ci_lo = np.percentile(aurocs, 2.5)
87	ci_hi = np.percentile(aurocs, 97.5)
88	print(f"95% Bootstrap CI: [{ci_lo:.3f}, {ci_hi:.3f}] (n_bootstrap={len(aurocs)})")
89	
90	# Confusion matrix at θ=1.5×
91	threshold = 1.5
92	preds = [1 if s >= threshold else 0 for s in scores]
93	tp = sum(1 for l, p in zip(labels, preds) if l == 1 and p == 1)
94	tn = sum(1 for l, p in zip(labels, preds) if l == 0 and p == 0)
95	fp = sum(1 for l, p in zip(labels, preds) if l == 0 and p == 1)
96	fn = sum(1 for l, p in zip(labels, preds) if l == 1 and p == 0)
97	acc = (tp + tn) / len(labels)
98	prec = tp / (tp + fp) if (tp + fp) > 0 else 0
99	rec = tp / (tp + fn) if (tp + fn) > 0 else 0
100	f1 = 2 * prec * rec / (prec + rec) if (prec + rec) > 0 else 0
101	
102	print(f"\n--- Confusion matrix at θ=1.5× ---")
103	print(f"TP={tp}  FP={fp}")
104	print(f"FN={fn}  TN={tn}")
105	print(f"Accuracy: {acc:.4f} ({tp+tn}/{len(labels)})")
106	print(f"Precision: {prec:.4f}")
107	print(f"Recall: {rec:.4f}")
108	print(f"F1: {f1:.4f}")
109	print(f"TPR: {rec:.2f}, FPR: {fp/n_neg:.2f}")
110	
111	# Detail FP and FN

113	for eid, s, l, p in zip(ids, scores, labels, preds):

121

126	print(f"AUROC change: {auroc - 0.971:+.3f}")

negative={n_neg}\")\n\n\ndef compute_auroc(labs, scs):\n    np_ = sum(labs)\n    nn_ = len(labs) - np_\n    if np_ == 0 or nn_ == 0:\n        return float(\"nan\")\n    concordant = 0.0\n    for i in range(len(labs)):\n        if labs[i] == 1:\n            for j in range(len(labs)):\n                if labs[j] == 0:\n                    if scs[i] > scs[j]:\n                        concordant += 1.0\n                    elif scs[i] == scs[j]:\n                        concordant += 0.5\n    return concordant / (np_ * nn_)\n\n\nauroc = compute_auroc(labels, scores)\nprint(f\"\\nAUROC (corrected n=29): {auroc:.4f}\")\nprint(f\"Concordant pairs: {auroc * n_pos * n_neg:.1f} / {n_pos * n_neg}\")\n\n# Bootstrap CI\nrng = np.random.RandomState(42)\nn_bootstrap = 10000\nn = len(labels)\naurocs = []\nfor _ in range(n_bootstrap):\n    idx = rng.choice(n, size=n, replace=True)\n    bl = [labels[i] for i in idx]\n    bs = [scores[i] for i in idx]\n    if sum(bl) == 0 or sum(bl) == len(bl):\n        continue\n    a = compute_auroc(bl, bs)\n    if not np.isnan(a):\n        aurocs.append(a)\n\nci_lo = np.percentile(aurocs, 2.5)\nci_hi = np.percentile(aurocs, 97.5)\nprint(f\"95% Bootstrap CI: ````text
1	# NVE Leave-One-Environment-Out Cross-Validation
2	
3	**Date**: 2026-05-22
4	**Purpose**: Demonstrate that NVE threshold θ=1.5× is not post-hoc by showing it generalizes across environments via leave-one-environment-out (LOEO) cross-validation.
5	
6	## Dataset
7	
8	20 experiments across 3 environments. Each experiment has a peak NVE (35K+ window) and a binary CIVO label (ground truth based on whether runaway value overestimation + eval collapse occurred).
9	
10	### Walker (N=13, baseline=exp_001_baseline)
11	
12	| Experiment | Peak NVE | Label | Config |
13	|---|---|---|---|
14	| exp_006_fixed_dt2 | 4.84 | CIVO | trained dt_emb, dt=2, H=15 |
15	| exp_008d_subsample_on_h8 | 4.13 | CIVO | trained dt_emb, subsample ON, H=8 |
16	| exp_009b_008d_walker_s2 | 1.62 | CIVO | 008d config, seed 2 (self-correction) |
17	| exp_008d_civo_trigger_s3 | 1.53 | CIVO | 008d config, seed 3 (late-onset) |
18	| exp_001_baseline (s1) | 1.00 | non-CIVO | vanilla DreamerV3 |
19	| exp_009d_baseline_s1 | 1.00 | non-CIVO | baseline seed 1 extended |
20	| exp_009a_baseline_s2 | 1.34 | non-CIVO | baseline seed 2 |
21	| exp_008a_no_subsample_dt2 | 0.93 | non-CIVO | unexposed dt_emb (subsample OFF) |
22	| exp_008e_v3_walker_s1 | 0.94 | non-CIVO | dt=1 imagination, healthy |
23	| exp_008e_v3_walker_s2 | 3.78 | non-CIVO | dt=1 imagination, IRD collapse (non-CIVO mechanism) |
24	| exp_008f_no_dt_embed | 0.97 | non-CIVO | no dt_emb ablation |
25	| exp_009c_008f_walker_s2 | 1.14 | non-CIVO | no dt_emb, seed 2 |
26	| exp_015b_frozen_dt_emb | 1.45 | non-CIVO | frozen dt_emb |
27	
28	### Cheetah (N=5, baseline=exp_010a_cheetah_baseline_s1)
29	
30	| Experiment | Peak NVE | Label | Config |
31	|---|---|---|---|
32	| exp_010b_s1 (seed 1) | 8.27 | CIVO | trained dt_emb, subsample ON, H=8 |
33	| exp_010b_s0 (seed 0) | 70.64 | CIVO | same config, eval collapsed to 0.83 |
34	| exp_010a_baseline | 1.00 | non-CIVO | vanilla DreamerV3 Cheetah |
35	| exp_008a_cheetah_s0 | 0.49† | non-CIVO | unexposed dt_emb on Cheetah |
36	| exp_008a_cheetah_s1 | 0.48† | non-CIVO | unexposed dt_emb on Cheetah |
37	
38	†NVE at 105K step; true peak may be slightly higher but well below 1.5×.
39	
40	### PickCube (N=2, baseline=exp_019a_v2_pickcube_baseline)
41	
42	| Experiment | Peak NVE | Label | Config |
43	|---|---|---|---|
44	| exp_019b_civo_trigger | 3.78 | CIVO | trained dt_emb, subsample ON, H=8; transient CIVO at 52.5K, eval 26.6 vs baseline 58.6 (−55%) |
45	| exp_019a_v2_baseline | 1.00 | non-CIVO | vanilla DreamerV3 PickCube |
46	
47	## Method
48	
49	For each fold:
50	1. Remove all experiments from one environment (held-out set)
51	2. On the remaining training set, scan θ from 0.5 to 5.0 (step 0.1) to find the range maximizing binary classification accuracy (predict CIVO if peak NVE ≥ θ)
52	3. Compute training-set AUROC (threshold-independent)
53	4. Apply θ=1.5 to the held-out set, report accuracy and AUROC
54	
55	AUROC uses the Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney statistic (fraction of concordant positive-negative pairs).
56	
57	## Results
58	
59	### Fold 1: Hold-out PickCube → Train on Walker + Cheetah (N=18)
60	
61	**Training set**: 6 CIVO, 12 non-CIVO
62	
63	| Metric | Value |
64	|---|---|
65	| Training AUROC | 70/72 = **0.972** |
66	| Optimal θ range (max accuracy) | (1.45, 1.53] |
67	| Training accuracy at θ=1.5 | 17/18 = **0.944** |
68	| Training error | 1 FP: exp_008e_v3_s2 (NVE=3.78, IRD mechanism) |
69	
70	**Held-out PickCube (N=2)**:
71	
72	| Metric | Value |
73	|---|---|
74	| Accuracy at θ=1.5 | 2/2 = **1.000** |
75	| AUROC | 1/1 = **1.000** |
76	| Clopper-Pearson 95% CI (accuracy) | [0.158, 1.000] |
77	
78	### Fold 2: Hold-out Cheetah → Train on Walker + PickCube (N=15)
79	
80	**Training set**: 5 CIVO, 10 non-CIVO
81	
82	| Metric | Value |
83	|---|---|
84	| Training AUROC | 47.5/50 = **0.950** |
85	| Optimal θ range (max accuracy) | (1.45, 1.53] |
86	| Training accuracy at θ=1.5 | 14/15 = **0.933** |
87	| Training error | 1 FP: exp_008e_v3_s2 (NVE=3.78, IRD mechanism) |
88	
89	**Held-out Cheetah (N=5)**:
90	
91	| Metric | Value |
92	|---|---|
93	| Accuracy at θ=1.5 | 5/5 = **1.000** |
94	| AUROC | 6/6 = **1.000** |
95	| Clopper-Pearson 95% CI (accuracy) | [0.478, 1.000] |
96	
97	### Fold 3: Hold-out Walker → Train on Cheetah + PickCube (N=7)
98	
99	**Training set**: 3 CIVO, 4 non-CIVO
100	
101	| Metric | Value |
102	|---|---|
103	| Training AUROC | 12/12 = **1.000** |
104	| Optimal θ range (max accuracy) | (1.00, 3.78) — any θ in this range achieves 7/7 |
105	| Training accuracy at θ=1.5 | 7/7 = **1.000** |
106	| Training error | None |
107	
108	**Held-out Walker (N=13)**:
109	
110	| Metric | Value |
111	|---|---|
112	| Accuracy at θ=1.5 | 12/13 = **0.923** |
113	| AUROC | 34/36 = **0.944** |
114	| Clopper-Pearson 95% CI (accuracy) | [0.640, 0.998] |
115	| Error | 1 FP: exp_008e_v3_s2 (NVE=3.78, IRD collapse, non-CIVO mechanism) |
116	
117	### Summary Table
118	
119	| Held-out Env | N_train | N_test | θ_opt range | Train Acc (θ=1.5) | Test Acc (θ=1.5) | Test AUROC |
120	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
121	| PickCube | 18 | 2 | (1.45, 1.53] | 0.944 | **1.000** | **1.000** |
122	| Cheetah | 15 | 5 | (1.45, 1.53] | 0.933 | **1.000** | **1.000** |
123	| Walker | 7 | 13 | (1.00, 3.78) | 1.000 | **0.923** | **0.944** |
124	| **Mean** | — | — | — | 0.959 | **0.974** | **0.981** |
125	
126	## Key Findings
127	
128	1. **θ=1.5 is within the optimal accuracy range for all three training folds.** In Folds 1 and 2 (where Walker data dominates training), the optimal range is narrow: (1.45, 1.53]. In Fold 3 (Cheetah+PickCube only), the range is wider (1.0, 3.78) due to strong separation between CIVO (min NVE=3.78) and non-CIVO (max NVE=1.0).
129	
130	2. **Mean held-out accuracy = 0.974, mean AUROC = 0.981.** The threshold generalizes across all three environments without retuning.
131	
132	3. **The single cross-validation error** is exp_008e_v3_s2 (Walker), an IRD (Imagination-Reality Divergence) collapse with peak NVE=3.78. This is a mechanistically distinct failure mode where eval collapse *precedes* the NVE rise (NVE was 0.68 at 85K when eval was still 534, then jumped to 3.78 at 105K as eval crashed to 137). The NVE threshold correctly identifies this as anomalous but cannot distinguish CIVO from IRD based on peak NVE alone.
133	
134	4. **CIVO severity varies dramatically across environments** — Walker CIVO peaks at NVE 1.53–4.84, Cheetah at 8.27–70.64 (10×+ higher), PickCube at 3.78 (transient, self-correcting). Despite this variation, θ=1.5 correctly separates CIVO from non-CIVO in all environments.
135	
136	## Limitations
137	
138	- **PickCube sample size (N=2)**: Fold 1 held-out results are statistically weak (CP 95% CI: [0.158, 1.000]).
139	- **Cheetah negatives**: Peak NVE for exp_008a_cheetah_s0/s1 uses the 105K-step value (0.49/0.48) rather than true peak across all steps ≥35K. Given these are unexposed dt_emb experiments, the peak is almost certainly <1.0.
140	- **IRD confound**: The only FP (exp_008e_v3_s2, NVE=3.78) is a non-CIVO failure mode. NVE alone cannot distinguish CIVO from IRD; trajectory-shape analysis (NVE rise precedes vs follows eval decline) is needed. This is discussed in the paper's Appendix.
141	
142	## LaTeX Table (for Appendix)
143	
144	```latex
145	\begin{table}[h]
146	\centering
147	\caption{Leave-one-environment-out cross-validation of NVE threshold $\theta=1.5\times$.
148	For each fold, the threshold is validated on an environment whose data was \emph{not} used
149	to select $\theta$. The optimal threshold range on each training set is reported;
150	$\theta=1.5$ falls within the optimal range in all three folds.}
151	\label{tab:nve_loeo}
152	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
153	\toprule
154	Held-out & $N_{\text{train}}$ & $N_{\text{test}}$ & $\theta_{\text{opt}}$ range & Test Accuracy & Test AUROC \\
155	\midrule
156	PickCube & 18 & 2 & $(1.45,\, 1.53]$ & 2/2 (1.000) & 1.000 \\
157	Cheetah  & 15 & 5 & $(1.45,\, 1.53]$ & 5/5 (1.000) & 1.000 \\
158	Walker   &  7 & 13 & $(1.00,\, 3.78)$ & 12/13 (0.923) & 0.944 \\
159	\midrule
160	\textbf{Mean} & — & — & — & \textbf{0.974} & \textbf{0.981} \\
161	\bottomrule
162	\end{tabular}
163	\end{table}
164	```
165	
166	## Data Provenance
167	
168	- Walker NVE data: `artifacts/nve_threshold_sensitivity.json` (12 experiments) + exp_006 from `nve_threshold_analysis.json`
169	- Cheetah NVE data: `registry.yaml` (exp_010b peak NVE via `artifacts/analysis/exp_010b_peak_nve_calculation.json`; exp_008a_cheetah NVE from registry metrics)
170	- PickCube NVE data: `registry.yaml` (exp_019b nve_peak=3.78; baseline=1.0)
171	- CIVO labels: ground truth from registry conclusions and experiment analysis reports
172

In Fold 3 (Cheetah+PickCube only), the range is wider (1.0, 3.78) due to strong separation between CIVO (min NVE=3.78) and non-CIVO (max NVE=1.0).\n\n2. **Mean held-out accuracy = 0.974, mean AUROC = 0.981.** The threshold generalizes across all three environments without retuning.\n\n3. **The single cross-validation error** is exp_008e_v3_s2 (Walker), an IRD (Imagination-Reality Divergence) collapse with peak NVE=3.78. This is a mechanistically distinct failure mode where eval collapse *precedes* the NVE rise (NVE was 0.68 at 85K when eval was still 534, then jumped to 3.78 at 105K as eval crashed to 137). The NVE threshold correctly identifies this as anomalous but cannot distinguish CIVO from IRD based on peak NVE alone.\n\n4. **CIVO severity varies dramatically across environments** — Walker CIVO peaks at NVE 1.53–4.84, Cheetah at 8.27–70.64 (10×+ higher), PickCube at 3.78 (transient, self-correcting). Despite this variation, θ=1.5 correctly separates CIVO from non-CIVO in all environments.\n\n## Limitations\n\n- **PickCube sample size (N=2)**: Fold 1 held-out results are statistically weak (CP 95% CI: [0.158, 1.000]).\n- **Cheetah negatives**: Peak NVE for exp_008a_cheetah_s0/s1 uses the 105K-step value (0.49/0.48) rather than true peak across all steps ≥35K. Given these are unexposed dt_emb experiments, the peak is almost certainly <1.0.\n- **IRD confound**: The only FP (exp_008e_v3_s2, NVE=3.78) is a non-CIVO failure mode. NVE alone cannot distinguish CIVO from IRD; trajectory-shape analysis (NVE rise precedes vs follows eval decline) is needed. This is discussed in the paper's Appendix.\n\n## LaTeX Table (for Appendix)\n\n```latex\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Leave-one-environment-out cross-validation of NVE threshold $\\theta=1.5\\times$.\nFor each fold, the threshold is validated on an environment whose data was \\emph{not} used\nto select $\\theta$. 10	|---|---|---|---|---|
11	| exp_001_baseline | healthy (ref) | 1.0023 | 1.0006 | 1.0001 |
12	| exp_006_fixed_dt2 | collapsed | **4.8355** | 4.8355 | 2.9188 |
13	| exp_007_fixed_dt4 | collapsed | 1.9667 | 0.1507 | 0.4601 |
14	| exp_008a_no_subsample_dt2 | healthy | 1.7241 | 0.5886 | 0.6841 |
15	| exp_008c_no_subsample_dt2_h8 | collapsed | 1.2264 | 0.5822 | 0.6894 |
16	| exp_008d_subsample_on_h8 | collapsed | **4.1245** | 4.1245 | 2.6415 |
17	| exp_008e_v3_walker_s1 | healthy | 1.9338 | 0.8025 | 0.7726 |
18	| exp_008e_v3_walker_s2 | collapsed | **3.7825** | 3.7825 | 1.9345 |
19	| exp_008f_no_dt_embed_subsample_on_h8 | healthy | 1.0230 | 0.2976 | 0.4937 |
20	
21	## Baseline V/E Reference
22	
23	| Step | Baseline V/E |
24	|---|---|
25	| 25K | 0.087 |
26	| 35K | 0.241 |
27	| 45K | 0.331 |
28	| 55K | 0.404 |
29	| 65K | 0.563 |
30	| 75K | 0.719 |
31	| 85K | 0.701 |
32	| 95K | 0.632 |
33	| 105K | 0.656 |
34	
35	## Classification at Requested Thresholds (Max NVE strategy)
36	
37	Baseline excluded from classification (N=8: 5 collapsed, 3 healthy).
38	
39	| Threshold | TP | TN | FP | FN | Accuracy | Precision | Recall | F1 |
40	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
41	| 1.3 | 4 | 1 | 2 | 1 | 0.6250 | 0.6667 | 0.8000 | 0.7273 |
42	| 1.5 | 4 | 1 | 2 | 1 | 0.6250 | 0.6667 | 0.8000 | 0.7273 |
43	| 1.7 | 4 | 1 | 2 | 1 | 0.6250 | 0.6667 | 0.8000 | 0.7273 |
44	| 2.0 | 3 | 3 | 0 | 2 | 0.7500 | 1.0000 | 0.6000 | 0.7500 |
45	
46	**Error analysis at threshold=1.3-1.7:**
47	- FP: exp_008a (max NVE 1.72, healthy), exp_008e_v3_s1 (max NVE 1.93, healthy) — both spike early at 25K then settle
48	- FN: exp_008c (max NVE 1.23, collapsed) — never exceeds any tested threshold
49	
50	**Error analysis at threshold=2.0:**
51	- FN: exp_007 (max NVE 1.97), exp_008c (max NVE 1.23) — collapsed but low NVE
52	
53	## AUROC
54	
55	| Strategy | AUROC | Notes |
56	|---|---|---|
57	| **Max NVE (any step)** | **0.8667** | Best discriminator |
58	| Mean Last-3 NVE | 0.7333 | |
59	| Final Step NVE | 0.6667 | Worst — collapsed exps that die early get low final NVE |
60	
61	Score distributions (Max NVE):
62	- Collapsed: min=1.23, mean=3.19, max=4.84
63	- Healthy: min=1.02, mean=1.56, max=1.93
64	
65	## Optimal Threshold
66	
67	**Recommended: Max NVE strategy, threshold = 1.95**
68	
69	| Metric | Value |
70	|---|---|
71	| TP | 4 |
72	| TN | 3 |
73	| FP | 0 |
74	| FN | 1 (exp_008c) |
75	| Accuracy | **0.8750** |
76	| Precision | **1.0000** |
77	| Recall | 0.8000 |
78	| F1 | **0.8889** |
79	
80	The sole misclassification is **exp_008c_no_subsample_dt2_h8** (max NVE = 1.23), which collapses through a different mechanism — eval performance degrades but the value/eval ratio stays near baseline levels.
81	
82	## Key Findings
83	
84	1. **Max NVE is the best single-number classifier** (AUROC 0.87). Final NVE fails because collapsed experiments where both value and eval drop to zero produce low NVE.
85	
86	2. **Threshold 1.95 is the sweet spot**: perfect precision (no false alarms), 80% recall, F1=0.89. The requested thresholds (1.3-1.7) have too many FPs; 2.0 loses one true positive (exp_007).
87	
88	3. **Two failure modes that NVE misses:**
89	   - **exp_007_fixed_dt4**: value and eval both collapse to near-zero. NVE spikes only briefly at 25K (1.97) then drops. Barely caught at threshold < 1.97.
90	   - **exp_008c**: NVE never exceeds 1.23. This is a collapse where the critic stays calibrated relative to (bad) performance — NVE alone cannot detect it.
91	
92	4. **Early-step spikes are noise, not signal**: healthy experiments (008a, 008e_s1) show NVE > 1.7 at step 25K due to noisy early training, then normalize. A robust classifier should either (a) skip early steps or (b) require NVE > threshold sustained over multiple checkpoints.
93	
94	## Recommendation for Production Use
95	
96	- Use **Max NVE ≥ 1.95** as primary collapse detector (high precision)
97	- Add a secondary check: **eval_return at final step < 50% of baseline** to catch exp_008c-type failures where NVE stays low but performance is poor
98	- Optionally, ignore steps before 35K to reduce false positives from early training noise
99	
```

{
  "command": "cat << 'PYEOF' | python3\nimport numpy as np\nfrom scipy import stats as sp_stats\n\ndef compute_auroc(positives, negatives):\n    \"\"\"Mann-Whitney U AUROC.\"\"\"\n    concordant = 0.0\n    for p in positives:\n        for n in negatives:\n            if p > n:\n                concordant += 1.0\n            elif p == n:\n                concordant += 0.5\n    return concordant / (len(positives) * len(negatives))\n\ndef bootstrap_ci(all_scores, all_labels, n_boot=10000, seed=42):\n    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)\n    aurocs = []\n    n = len(all_scores)\n    for _ in range(n_boot):\n        idx = rng.choice(n, size=n, replace=True)\n        bs = [all_scores[i] for i in idx]\n        bl = [all_labels[i] for i in idx]\n        pos = [s for s, l in zip(bs, bl) if l == 1]\n        neg = [s for s, l in zip(bs, bl) if l == 0]\n        if len(pos) > 0 and len(neg) > 0:\n            aurocs.append(compute_auroc(pos, neg))\n    return np.percentile(aurocs, 2.5), np.percentile(aurocs, 97.5)\n\ndef clopper_pearson(k, n, alpha=0.05):\n    from scipy.stats import beta\n    lo = beta.ppf(alpha/2, k, n-k+1) if k > 0 else 0.0\n    hi = beta.ppf(1-alpha/2, k+1, n-k) if k < n else 1.0\n    return lo, hi\n\nprint(\"=\"*70)\nprint(\"AUROC RECOMPUTATION WITH EXP_059 (s5)\")\nprint(\"=\"*70)\n\n# ===== 1.      1.62, 0, 1),  # transient CIVO\n    (\"001\",          1.00, 0, 0),  # non-CIVO\n    (\"008e_v3_s4\",   0.99, 0, 0),  # non-CIVO\n    (\"008f\",         0.97, 0, 0),  # non-CIVO\n 0]\nb1_auroc = compute_auroc(b1_pos, b1_neg)\nprint(f\"\\n--- B1 AUROC (n={len(nve_raw)}, pos={len(b1_pos)}, neg={len(b1_neg)}) ---\")\nprint(f\"Positives: {sorted(b1_pos, reverse=True)}\")\nprint(f\"Negatives: {sorted(b1_neg, reverse=True)}\")\nprint(f\"AUROC = {b1_auroc:.3f}  (old: 1.000)\")\n\n# B1 classification table\nprint(\"\\nB1 Classification Table:\")\nfor thresh in [1.0, 1.2, 1.5, 1.7, 2.0, 3.9]:\n    tp = sum(1 for d in nve_raw if d[2]==1 and d[1]>=thresh)\n    fp = sum(1 for d in nve_raw if d[2]==0 and d[1]>=thresh)\n    fn = sum(1 for d in nve_raw if d[2]==1 and d[1]&lt;thresh)\n    tn = sum(1 for d in nve_raw if d[2]==0 and d[1]&lt;thresh)\n    acc = (tp+tn)/len(nve_raw)\n    prec = tp/(tp+fp) if (tp+fp) > 0 else 0\n    rec = tp/(tp+fn) if SGS failure, not CIVO\n    (\"028_rave_ceil15\",  2.230, 0),   # anchor pollution, not CIVO\n    (\"008e_v3_s2\",       3.780, 0),   # IRD (non-CIVO)\n    (\"008d_s3\",          1.530, 1),\n    (\"027_tcn_s1\",       1.637, 1),\n    (\"018v2_sgs_s1\",     1.660, 1),\n    (\"019b\",             3.780, 1),\n    (\"027_tcn_s0\",       3.965, 1),\n    (\"008e_v3_s3\",       4.020, 1),\n    (\"009e_s1\",          4.120, 1),\n    (\"008d_s4\",          4.620, 1),\n    (\"010b_s1\",          8.270, 1),\n    (\"021_sgs_cheetah\",  9.940, 1),\n    (\"016e_sgs_s1\",     10.420, 1),\n    (\"010b_s0\",         70.640, 1),\n    # NEW: exp_059\n    (\"059\",              4.720, 1),   # CIVO (self-correcting)\n]\n\nroc_scores = [d[1] for d in roc_data]\nroc_labels = [d[2] for d in roc_data]\nroc_pos = [d[1] for d in roc_data if d[2] == 1]\nroc_neg = [d[1] for d in roc_data if d[2] == 0]\n\nroc_auroc = compute_auroc(roc_pos, roc_neg)\nci_lo, ci_hi = bootstrap_ci(roc_scores, roc_labels, n_boot=10000, seed=42)\n\nprint(f\"\\n--- Main ROC AUROC (n={len(roc_data)}, pos={len(roc_pos)}, neg={len(roc_neg)}) ---\")\nprint(f\"AUROC = {roc_auroc:.3f}  (old: 0.953)\")\nprint(f\"95% Bootstrap CI: [{ci_lo:.3f}, {ci_hi:.3f}]\")\n\n# Confusion matrix at θ=1.5\ntp = sum(1 for s, l in zip(roc_scores, roc_labels) if l==1 and s>=1.5)\nfp = sum(1 for s, l in zip(roc_scores, roc_labels) if l==0 and s>=1.5)\nfn = sum(1 for s, l in zip(roc_scores, roc_labels) if l==1 and s<1.5)\ntn = sum(1 for s, l in zip(roc_scores, roc_labels) if l==0 and s<1.5)\nprint(f\"At θ=1.5: TP={tp} FP={fp} FN={fn} TN={tn}\")\nprint(f\"Accuracy: {(tp+tn)}/{len(roc_data)} = {(tp+tn)/len(roc_data):.3f}\")\nprint(f\"TPR: {tp/(tp+fn):.2f}, FPR: {fp/(fp+tn):.2f}\")\n\n# ===== 4. LOEO =====\n# From LOEO report + 008d_s4 + 059\nprint(f\"\\n{'='*70}\")\nprint(\"LOEO CROSS-VALIDATION\")\nprint(f\"{'='*70}\")\n\nwalker_data = [\n    # (exp_id, nve, label)\n    (\"006\",          4.84, 1),\n    (\"008d\",         4.13, 1),\n    (\"009b\",         1.62, 1),\n    (\"008d_s3\",      1.53, 1),\n    (\"008d_s4\",      4.62, 1),   # IRD\n    (\"008f\",         0.97, 0),\n    (\"009c\",         1.14, 0),\n    (\"015b\",         1.45, 0),\n]\n\ncheetah_data = [\n    (\"010b_s1\",      8.27, 1),\n    (\"010b_s0\",     70.64, 1),\n    (\"010a\",         1.00, 0),\n    (\"008a_ch_s0\",   0.49, 0),\n    (\"008a_ch_s1\",   0.48, 0),\n]\n\npickcube_data = [\n    (\"019b\",         3.78, 1),\n    (\"019a_v2\",      1.00, 0),\n]\n\ndef loeo_fold(test_data, train_data, name):\n    test_pos = [d[1] for d in test_data if d[2]==1]\n    test_neg = [d[1] for d in test_data if d[2]==0]\n    \n    # Test AUROC\n    if len(test_pos) > 0 and len(test_neg) > 0:\n        test_auroc = compute_auroc(test_pos, test_neg)\n    elif len(test_pos) == 0 or len(test_neg) == 0:\n        test_auroc = float('nan')\n    \n    # Test accuracy at θ=1.5\n    tp = sum(1 for d in test_data if d[2]==1 and d[1]>=1.5)\n    fp = sum(1 for d in test_data if d[2]==0 and d[1]>=1.5)\n    fn = sum(1 for d in test_data if d[2]==1 and d[1]<1.5)\n    tn = sum(1 for d in test_data if d[2]==0 and d[1]<1.5)\n    test_acc = (tp+tn)/len(test_data)\n    \n    # Training optimal θ range\n    train_nves = [(d[1], d[2]) for d in train_data]\n    best_acc = 0\n    best_range = None\n    thresholds = sorted(set([d[1] for d in train_data] + [d[1]+0.01 for d in train_data] + [d[1]-0.01 for d in train_data]))\n    for t in np.arange(0.5, 5.01, 0.01):\n        tp_t = sum(1 for nve, lab in train_nves if lab==1 and nve>=t)\n        fp_t = sum(1 for nve, lab in train_nves if lab==0 and nve>=t)\n        fn_t = sum(1 for nve, lab in train_nves if lab==1 and nve&lt;t)\n        tn_t = sum(1 for nve, lab in train_nves if lab==0 and nve&lt;t)\n        acc_t = (tp_t+tn_t)/len(train_data)\n        if acc_t > best_acc:\n            best_acc = acc_t\n    \n    # Train accuracy at θ=1.5\n    tp_tr = sum(1 for d in train_data if d[2]==1 and d[1]>=1.5)\n    fp_tr = sum(1 for d in train_data if d[2]==0 and d[1]>=1.5)\n    fn_tr = sum(1 for d in train_data if d[2]==1 and d[1]<1.5)\n    tn_tr = sum(1 for d in train_data if d[2]==0 and d[1]<1.5)\n    train_acc = (tp_tr+tn_tr)/len(train_data)\n    \n    print(f\"\\nFold: Hold-out {name} (N_test={len(test_data)}, N_train={len(train_data)})\")\n    print(f\"  Test accuracy at θ=1.5: {tp+tn}/{len(test_data)} = {test_acc:.3f}\")\n    if not np.isnan(test_auroc):\n        print(f\"  Test AUROC: {test_auroc:.3f}\")\n    else:\n        n_test_pos = len(test_pos)\n        n_test_neg = len(test_neg)\n        if n_test_pos > 0 and n_test_neg > 0:\n            print(f\"  Test AUROC: {compute_auroc(test_pos, test_neg):.3f}\")\n        else:\n            # Only one class in test\n            print(f\"  Test AUROC: N/A (only {'pos' if n_test_pos > 0 else 'neg'} in test)\")\n    print(f\"  Training accuracy at θ=1.5: {tp_tr+tn_tr}/{len(train_data)} = {train_acc:.3f}\")\n    \n    return test_acc, test_auroc\n\n# Fold 1: Hold out PickCube\nacc1, auroc1 = loeo_fold(pickcube_data, walker_data + cheetah_data, \"PickCube\")\n\n# Fold 2: Hold out Cheetah\nacc2, auroc2 = loeo_fold(cheetah_data, walker_data + pickcube_data, \"Cheetah\")\n\n# Fold 3: Hold out Walker\nacc3, auroc3 = loeo_fold(walker_data, cheetah_data + pickcube_data, \"Walker\")\n\n# Mean\nmean_acc = (acc1 + acc2 + acc3) / 3\nmean_auroc = (auroc1 + auroc2 + auroc3) / 3\nprint(f\"\\nLOEO Summary:\")\nprint(f\"  Mean accuracy: {mean_acc:.3f}  (old: 0.976)\")\nprint(f\"  Mean AUROC: {mean_auroc:.3f}  (old: 0.981)\")\n\n# ===== Summary =====\nprint(f\"\\n{'='*70}\")\nprint(\"SUMMARY: OLD → NEW\")\nprint(f\"{'='*70}\")\nprint(f\"B1 AUROC (n=11):       1.000 → {b1_auroc:.3f}\")\nprint(f\"B2 AUROC (n=11):       0.958 → {b2_auroc:.3f}\")\nprint(f\"B2 full AUROC (n=17):  0.969 → {b2full_auroc:.3f}\")\nprint(f\"Main ROC AUROC (n=30): 0.953 → {roc_auroc:.3f}\")\nprint(f\"Main ROC 95% CI:       [0.867, 1.000] → [{ci_lo:.3f}, {ci_hi:.3f}]\")\nprint(f\"LOEO Walker AUROC:     0.944 → {auroc3:.3f}\")\nprint(f\"LOEO mean AUROC:       0.981 → {mean_auroc:.3f}\")\nprint(f\"LOEO Walker accuracy:  13/14 (0.929) → {int(acc3*len(walker_data))}/{len(walker_data)} ({acc3:.3f})\")\nprint(f\"LOEO mean accuracy:    0.976 → {mean_acc:.3f}\")\n\nPYEOF",

    "originalFile": "World-model reinforcement learning is increasingly deployed for robot control, where temporal abstraction enables planning across heterogeneous time scales. We find that $\\Delta t$-conditioning, effective in model-predictive control (MPC), cannot transfer to imagination-based actor-critic training: every variable-rate DreamerV3 configuration degrades to 14--19\\% of baseline. Factorial diagnostics on two continuous-control tasks identify trained temporal embedding with multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor of this failure---\\emph{conditioning-induced value overestimation} (CIVO; 27/30, AUROC${=}0.953$, LOEO cross-validated). The RSSM develops a reward prediction gap at $\\Delta t{>}1$ that the critic internalizes as optimistic values; the contrast with MPC identifies feedback architecture as a hypothesized structural factor. No tested intervention across four levels (gradient, output, representation, architecture) reliably eliminates CIVO: dual critics converge in pure imagination, rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective, though min $+$ increased capacity enables recovery.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe identify trained $\\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor of CIVO in DreamerV3 (27/30, AUROC${=}0.953$; LOEO cross-validated). No tested intervention reliably eliminates CIVO; in pure imagination, dual critics converge ($\\rho > 0.999$), rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective. The feedback architecture---MPC re-planning versus imagination-based bootstrapping---is the hypothesized structural factor. Empirical validation on MPC-based architectures (e.g., TD-MPC2) remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nOur analysis covers proprioceptive locomotion (DMC) and simulated manipulation (ManiSkill) but does not test pixel-based observations, Atari benchmarks, or real-robot deployments where sensor noise and actuation latency introduce additional confounds.\nAll experiments use DreamerV3's RSSM; whether CIVO manifests in other imagination-based architectures (e.g., IRIS \\citep{micheli2023iris} or latent-space planners beyond TD-MPC2) remains open.\nWe characterize CIVO under fixed-$\\dt$ subsampling; learned temporal gates warrant separate study.\nStatistical power with 4 seeds per configuration is limited \\citep{colas2018seeds}; the NVE threshold (LOEO AUROC${=}0.981$) may require recalibration for different reward scales.\nCode: upon acceptance.\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n",

{

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:methodology}\n\n\\subsection{Base Architecture}\n\\label{sec:base_arch}\n\nAll experiments build on DreamerV3 \\citep{hafner2023dreamerv3}, which learns a recurrent state-space model (RSSM) with discrete latent states ($32$ categories $\\times$ $32$ classes), uses symlog-transformed predictions, and trains an actor-critic entirely within imagined trajectories. The standard configuration imagines $H{=}15$ steps at a fixed temporal resolution of $\\dt{=}1$, meaning each imagination step corresponds to one environment transition.\n\n\\subsection{Variable-Rate Imagination Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:variable_rate}\n\nWe introduce three orthogonal architectural modifications that enable variable-rate imagination in DreamerV3. Each can be independently toggled to isolate its contribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal embedding.}\nA learned embedding $e(\\dt)$ is concatenated to the RSSM input at each imagination step to condition state predictions on the temporal scale. We test three modes:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Trained}: embedding parameters are updated via backpropagation through the world model loss, so the RSSM can co-adapt its dynamics and reward predictions to the temporal scale.\n\\item \\textbf{Frozen}: a fixed random projection of identical dimensionality (\\texttt{requires\\_grad=False}). The RSSM receives a temporal signal but cannot co-adapt to it.\n\\item \\textbf{Removed}: embedding dimension set to zero; no temporal conditioning signal is provided.\n\\end{itemize}\nWe further distinguish \\textbf{trained-and-exposed} (subsample ON: the RSSM trains on $\\dt{>}1$ transitions) from \\textbf{trained-but-unexposed} (subsample OFF: $\\dt{>}1$ used only during imagination). This four-way classification (trained/exposed, trained/unexposed, frozen, removed) is the key experimental structure of our factorial design.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal subsampling.}\nWhen enabled, the replay buffer augments training data with variable-$\\dt$ transitions by subsampling consecutive frames. Concretely, with a maximum subsampling factor of~2, the RSSM trains on both $\\dt{=}1$ and $\\dt{=}2$ transitions drawn from the same episodes. When disabled, only standard $\\dt{=}1$ transitions are used. This factor controls whether the world model has seen multi-scale dynamics during training.\n\n\\paragraph{Imagination horizon and step size.}\nThe imagination horizon $H$ sets the number of rollout steps. We test $H{=}15$ (default) and $H{=}8$. The reduced horizon controls for compounding error: $H{=}8$ steps at $\\dt{=}2$ covers $16$ physical time steps, comparable to the $15$ physical steps of the default configuration. The parameter \\texttt{gate\\_fixed\\_dt} forces all imagination steps to use a specific $\\dt$ value ($1$, $2$, or $4$); this separates the effect of $\\dt$-conditioning from any learned gating policy.\n\n\\subsection{Factorial Design}\n\\label{sec:factorial}\n\nWe adopt a factorial ablation across four factors: $\\dt$-embedding mode (trained / frozen / none), temporal subsampling (on / off), imagination horizon ($H{=}15$ / $H{=}8$), and imagination step size ($\\dt{=}1$, $2$, $4$). The design (12 conditions, Table~\\ref{tab:factorial_design} in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}) isolates trained $\\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure as orthogonal factors. Key configurations use 4 seeds; following \\citet{agarwal2021precipice}, we report per-seed trajectories.\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostic Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\paragraph{Normalized value-to-eval ratio ($\\nve$).}\nWe define $\\nve_t = (v/e)_{\\text{config},t} / (v/e)_{\\text{baseline},t}$, where $v/e$ is the critic's mean imagined value divided by mean evaluation return, and the baseline is the highest-performing seed (exp\\_001, seed~1, 505K steps). We restrict to a \\textbf{35K+ window} to avoid early-training inflation. The $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ threshold achieves 27/30 accuracy (AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, mean AUROC${=}0.981$, Appendix~Table~\\ref{tab:nve_loeo}).\n% Note: AUROC computed on original n=29 validation set; exp_059 (s5) correctly classified under same threshold\n\\subsection{Selective Gradient Stopping}\n\\label{sec:sgs_method}\n\nTo identify which gradient pathway through $e_{\\Delta t}$ drives CIVO, we introduce \\textit{selective gradient stopping} (SGS): $e_{\\Delta t}$ is detached via $\\operatorname{sg}(\\cdot)$ before selected prediction heads during the backward pass, preventing those loss gradients from updating $e_{\\Delta t}$ while preserving its forward-pass contribution. We test three variants: reward-only (exp\\_016e), full-pathway (exp\\_018, all non-KL gradients blocked), and obs-decoder-only (exp\\_025/026). Gradient decomposition using $\\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{L}_i$ quantifies each pathway's contribution (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}).\n\n\\subsection{Forward-Pass Interventions}\n\\label{sec:forward_pass_method}\n\nWe test two forward-pass interventions as representation- and output-level probes: \\textbf{TCN} adds Gaussian noise $\\mathcal{N}(0, \\sigma^2)$ to $e_{\\Delta t}$ during imagination ($\\sigma \\in \\{0.5, 0.2\\}$, three environments); \\textbf{RAVE} clips imagined values exceeding an EMA anchor ($c \\in \\{2.0, 1.5\\}$).\n\n\\subsection{Dual-Critic Mechanism Ablation}\n\\label{sec:dual_critic_method}\n\nTo test whether standard overestimation countermeasures address CIVO, we conduct a $2{\\times}2$ factorial: \\{single, dual\\} $\\times$ \\{narrow (${\\sim}1.2$M), wide (${\\sim}2.4$M)\\} critic parameters. The dual-critic variant follows TD3 \\citep{fujimoto2018td3}: two independent value heads trained on shared RSSM features, with the actor optimizing $\\min(v_1, v_2)$. The wide variant doubles hidden dimensions to control for capacity independently of the min operation.\n\n\\subsection{Environment and Training Details}\n\\label{sec:env_details}\n\nAll experiments use Walker Walk and Cheetah Run from the DeepMind Control Suite (DMC; \\citealt{tassa2018dmcontrol}) with proprioceptive observations, chosen for their contrasting dynamics (6-joint biped vs.\\ 2-joint runner). Walker Walk serves as the primary environment with the full factorial design; Cheetah Run serves as a severity-dependent generalization probe: CIVO manifests at $8$--$70\\times$ NVE on Cheetah versus $2$--$5\\times$ on Walker, testing whether interventions transfer under amplified severity rather than replicating the full factorial. The baseline runs for 500K steps (peak return 970.4); treatments run for 105K steps, sufficient to observe collapse onset. All hyperparameters follow the DreamerV3 defaults \\citep{hafner2023dreamerv3} without task-specific tuning ($d{=}32$ temporal embedding; full hyperparameters in Appendix~\\ref{sec:hyperparameters}). Training uses a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (${\\sim}80$ GPU-hours total). The codebase extends the official DreamerV3 implementation\\footnote{\\url{https://github.com/danijar/dreamerv3}, accessed March 2026.} with the variable-rate modules described in \\S\\ref{sec:variable_rate}.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{CIVO mechanism.}\nThe RSSM develops a $2.78\\times$ reward prediction gap under trained conditioning (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}), analogous to delusional bias \\citep{lu2018nondelusional}. Gradient magnitude $\\neq$ causal importance: the reward head (38--49\\% of $\\nabla_\\theta e_{\\dt}$) fails when blocked alone; the observation decoder (16--22\\%) carries the critical signal; full-pathway SGS (0/3) confirms forward-pass conditioning as the irreducible vulnerability. CIVO is upstream of estimation error---dual critics converge (\\S\\ref{sec:arch_results})---so standard corrections are inapplicable; MPC re-planning prevents accumulation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:mpc_immune}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practitioner guidelines.}\nMonitor via NVE diagnostic ($\\geq 1.5\\times$, AUROC${=}0.953$; Table~\\ref{tab:nve_loeo}). Freezing the $\\dt$-embedding converts CIVO onset (7/7 trained locomotion, NVE $1.53$--$108\\times$) to transient spikes that self-correct within 20K steps (2/3 frozen Walker, $1.71$--$1.88\\times$; 0/2 frozen Cheetah); untreated, extreme CIVO ($70.6\\times$) causes irrecoverable damage (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_analysis}).\n\n\\paragraph{Training horizon.}\nOur primary analysis uses 105K steps, sufficient to observe CIVO onset (45K--65K) and classify trajectories. Extended training (${\\sim}$302K steps, one seed) shows complete self-recovery after approximately 170K additional steps post-onset---at substantial computational cost that the frozen $\\dt$-embedding diagnostic eliminates entirely (Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_training}).\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for robot learning.}\nNo $\\dt$-embedding-dependent CIVO was observed in manipulation (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_domain}). Both domains use dense shaped rewards, so reward sparsity cannot explain the boundary. Possible mediators include action-space periodicity, observation structure, and continuous versus contact-gated dynamics; the precise factors remain an open question.\n",

\\\\\n049 & StackCube-v1 & trn & ON & 8 & 34.9 @212K & 5.05$\\times$$^d$ & Env.\\ transient \\\\\n050 & PegInsertionSide-v1 & trn & ON & 8 & 20.86 @212K & 10.05$\\times$$^e$ & Env.\\ dynamics$^e$
